===== Lai2026_Nature_BaseEditing-BetaThalassaemia =====

Nature  |  Vol 653  |  21 May 2026  |  923
Article
Clinical application of base editing for treating 
β-thalassaemia
Yongrong Lai1,2,3,11 ✉, Rongrong Liu1,2,3,11, Lijie Wang4,5,11, Xu-Kai Ma6,11, Yaliang Li5, Gaohui Yang1,2,3, 
Lingling Shi1,2,3, Yi-Lin Guo6, Zhenbin Wei1,2,3, Xuemei Zhou1,2,3, Wenchao Xu4, Yaofeng Hou4,7, 
Annarita Miccio8, Bei Yang4,7,9, Xiaodun Mou5 ✉, Li Yang6 ✉ & Jia Chen4,9,10 ✉
β-Thalassaemia is caused by reduced or absent production of β-haemoglobin1–4. 
Previously, we performed laboratory-scale electroporation of CD34+ haematopoietic 
stem and progenitor cells from patients with β-thalassaemia using a transformer  
base editor5,6. The aim was to target the binding motif of the transcription repressor 
BCL11A in the HBG1 and HBG2 promoters7 to reactivate fetal haemoglobin (HbF) 
production. Here we present results of a phase 1 clinical trial (ClinicalTrials.gov 
identifier: NCT06024876) of five patients who received autologous CD34+ cells 
modified using a transformer base editor at clinical scale (CS-101). With a median 
follow-up of 23.0 months after CS-101 infusion, the median times to neutrophil and 
platelet engraftment were 16 days and 25 days, respectively. Moreover, all patients 
had stopped red blood cell transfusions, with a median time to the last transfusion  
of 18 days after CS-101 infusion. The mean total haemoglobin and HbF concentrations 
were 12.4 ± 1.0 and 11.5 ± 0.9 g dl–1, respectively, at month 3 after infusion. These levels 
remained at similar or higher levels throughout the follow-up period, which indicated 
rapid haematopoietic reconstitution. The adverse events of CS-101 were generally 
consistent with those of busulfan myeloablative conditioning and autologous 
haematopoietic stem and progenitor cell transplantation. No deaths or cancer 
occurrences were reported. In summary, CS-101 can lead to rapid and sustained 
increases in both total haemoglobin and HbF levels, which resulted in early and 
enduring transfusion independence.
β-Thalassaemia, a prevalent group of monogenic diseases, affects 
more than 40,000 children annually worldwide1–4. β-Thalassaemia 
is caused by reduced or absent haemoglobin β-globin chain synthe-
sis, which leads to an imbalance of α-globin to non-α-globin (α/non- 
α-globin) chains and an excess of α-globin chains1–4. The accumulation 
of excess α-globin chains can form toxic aggregates that results in 
anaemia, ineffective erythropoiesis and dysregulation of iron metabo-
lism1–4. The severity of β-thalassaemia correlates with the degree of 
α/non-α-globin chain imbalance, and for those with severe forms, such 
as transfusion-dependent β-thalassaemia (TDT), lifelong red blood 
cell (RBC) transfusions are required for survival1,2,8. Current standard 
treatments for TDT involve regular RBC transfusions and iron chela-
tion to manage iron overload1–3,8. Allogeneic haematopoietic stem 
cell (HSC) transplantation represents a potentially curative option. 
However, its broad application faces challenges, including the short-
age of human leukocyte-antigen-matched donors, graft rejection, 
graft-versus-host disease and the risk of transplantation-related 
death2,3.
So far, more than 350 mutations that cause β-thalassaemia have 
been documented3,9. Gene-addition therapy using lentiviral vectors 
to introduce the β-globin gene (HBB) structure has shown efficacy in 
achieving transfusion independence in patients with the non-β0/β0 
genotype and in patients with the β0/β0 genotype (who do not have 
haemoglobin levels as high as those in patients with non-β0/β0)10–14. 
Alternatively, an increased level of HbF, which is composed of two 
α-globin and two γ-globin chains, is positively associated with reduced 
morbidity and mortality of patients with β-thalassaemia15–18. Thus, 
reactivating the expression of γ-globin chains, which is expressed in the 
fetus but is gradually silenced after birth19,20, can be another universal 
strategy for treating β-thalassaemia21. CRISPR–Cas9 nuclease-mediated 
editing in the erythroid-specific enhancer region of BCL11A, the gene 
that encodes a major transcription repressor for the γ-globin genes 
HBG1 and HBG2 (HBG1/2) in adults15,19,22, has been used to reactivate the 
expression of γ-globin16,23,24 to treat TDT. Moreover, this strategy can 
lead to transfusion independence in patients with the non-β0/β0 or β0/β0 
genotype25,26 with long-term efficacy27. However, preclinical studies

924  |  Nature  |  Vol 653  |  21 May 2026
Article
have shown that the generation of DNA double-strand breaks (DSBs) 
by CRISPR–Cas9 nuclease, which is essential for the targeted editing 
process, can trigger the p53-dependent DNA damage response. This 
leads to cell cycle arrest and apoptosis28,29 and the risk of undesirable 
chromosomal abnormalities30–32.
Base editing can induce targeted base conversions without the 
need for DSB generation, thereby largely avoiding the cell toxicity 
and chromosomal abnormalities related to DSBs33–39. We previously 
used a high-precision transformer base editor (tBE)5,6 to modify the 
BCL11A-binding motif in the promoter regions of HBG1/2 in haemat-
opoietic stem and progenitor cells (HSPCs) from both healthy donors 
and patients with β-thalassaemia. tBE-mediated base editing robustly 
reactivated HbF production with low levels of cell toxicity7 (Fig. 1a). 
Here we describe the evaluation of the safety and efficacy of CS-101, 
an ex vivo tBE-mediated autologous HSPC therapy for β-thalassaemia, 
in patients with TDT. CS-101 treatment resulted in early neutrophil and 
platelet engraftment, a short duration of post-infusion RBC transfusion 
support and high levels of HbF reactivation.
Preclinical studies of tBE efficacy
In our previous study, we used a truncated helper single-guide RNA 
(sgRNA)—MS2-aptamer-containing helper sgRNA (hsgHBG-MS2), with 
a 10-nucleotide spacer region—to recruit a mouse APOBEC3 cytidine 
deaminase (mA3CDA1) fused with a cleavable deoxycytidine deaminase 
inhibitor from mouse APOBEC3 (mA3dCDI). To this construct, a main 
sgRNA—boxB-aptamer-containing main sgRNA (msgHBG-boxB), with 
a 20-nucleotide spacer region—was added to recruit a split version of 
the TEV protease7 (Fig. 1b). At the on-target site (the BCL11A-binding 
motif in the promoter regions of HBG1/2), mA3dCDI can be cleaved 
from the fused mA3CDA1 (Fig. 1b). Subsequently, mA3CDA1 can induce 
efficient C-to-T editing (Fig. 1b). Because colocalization of hsgHBG-MS2 
and msgHBG-boxB occurs only at the on-target site, mA3dCDI remains 
fused to mA3CDA1 elsewhere, which inhibits mA3CDA1 activity and 
prevents the occurrence of off-target mutations5–7.
We first assessed the clinical-scale efficiency of tBE-mediated editing 
in manufactured CD34+ HSPCs from five healthy donors. The mean 
base-editing frequency (the percentage of sequencing reads with edited 
BCL11A-binding motifs, including TGAD–115C–114A, TGAC–115D–114A, and 
TGAD–115D–114A, where D = A, G or T) was 62.1% (Fig. 1c). Analyses of 
the frequencies of small nucleotide insertions or deletions (indels) 
in the edited HSPCs showed that tBE induced minimal levels of small 
indels (Extended Data Fig. 1a). After in vitro erythroid differentiation, 
the mean percentage of HbF increased to 45.3% compared with 13.7% 
in the untreated control samples (Fig. 1d, Extended Data Fig. 1b and 
Supplementary Table 1), and the mean percentage of F-cells (RBCs 
expressing HbF) increased to 44.9% compared with 20% in the untreated 
control samples (Fig. 1e and Extended Data Fig. 1c). Notably, around 
60% editing efficiency in HSPCs (Fig. 1c) resulted in about 40% F-cells 
(Fig. 1e), a value similar to what was achieved in our previous study7. 
These phenomena could be partially due to the inefficiency of in vitro 
erythroid differentiation, in which not all edited HSPCs successfully 
differentiate into F-cells. Meanwhile, we used our clinical-scale manu-
facturing process to further edit primary patient HSPCs and examined 
p53 activation and cell cycle effects. Compared with Cas9 nuclease 
editing, tBE-mediated editing resulted in lower induction of P21 mRNA, 
which is a well-established downstream marker of p53-mediated cell 
cycle arrest and apoptosis40 (Extended Data Fig. 1d).
We also transplanted CD34+ HSPCs from healthy donors into immu-
nocompromised mice. After 26 weeks, no significant differences were 
observed between the edited and the untreated HSPCs in terms of 
engraftment and differentiation potential (Fig. 1f,g and Extended Data 
Fig. 2). The base-editing efficiency was largely maintained (around 40%) 
in the bone marrow and peripheral blood of immunocompromised mice 
and was stabilized at 4 weeks after transplantation (Fig. 1h).
We next investigated whether tBE induced off-target editing in 
gDNA. Using Cas-OFFinder41 and Digested-genome sequencing 
(Digenome-seq)42, we identified 123 potential off-target sites: 87 by 
Cas-OFFinder and additional 36 by Digenome-seq. No obvious off-target 
editing was observed at these sites in edited CD34+ HSPCs from the five 
healthy donors (Fig. 1i).
Furthermore, we performed a dose-dependent comparison of 
tBE-mediated and hA3A-BE3-mediated base editing, as hA3A-BE3 is a 
standard cytosine base editor with high editing potency at the HBG1/2 
promoters7,43. These were tested alongside Cas9-nuclease-mediated 
gene editing of the BCL11A erythroid enhancer in HSPCs from a patient 
with the β0/β+ genotype. After in vitro erythroid differentiation, the 
percentages of HbF and F-cells were both positively correlated 
with tBE-mediated base-editing frequencies. The steeper slopes of 
tBE-mediated and hA3A-BE3-mediated editing in the HBG1/2 promoter 
region also demonstrated more robust HbF production than with the 
Cas9 nuclease strategy for targeting the BCL11A erythroid enhancer 
(Fig. 2a,b and Extended Data Fig. 3). This result was consistent with 
previous findings when cells from healthy donors were used7. We also 
investigated off-target editing in tBE-mediated editing of CD34+ HSPCs 
from patients, and no obvious off-target editing was detected (Fig. 2c).
In addition to editing cytosines in the BCL11A-binding motif of the 
HBG1/2 promoters, other base-editing strategies have demonstrated 
therapeutic potential. To compare their performance, we conducted a 
side-by-side evaluation of five representative base-editing approaches 
using HSPCs from two patients (Extended Data Fig. 4). The base-editing 
strategies included the two cytosine base-editor-mediated platforms 
described above (Fig. 2a) and hA3A(N57Q)-BE3, which targets the 
BCL11A erythroid enhancer24. We also tested two adenine base editor 
(ABE)-mediated strategies for reactivating HbF expression: ABE8e 
targeting the CAGATA–175 motif in the HBG1/2 promoters44 and ABE8e 
targeting both the BCL11A-binding motif of the HBG1/2 promoters and 
the BCL11A erythroid enhancer45.
When targeting the BCL11A-binding motif of the HBG1/2 promoters, 
both tBE and hA3A-BE3 achieved around 75% editing efficiency, whereas 
ABE8e exceeded 90%. When targeting the BCL11A erythroid enhancer, 
hA3A(N57Q)-BE3 and ABE8e each achieved high editing efficiencies 
(>90%). ABE8e-NG, which recognizes NG protospacer adjacent motifs 
(PAMs), targeting the CAGATA-175 motif in the HBG1/2 promoters showed 
relatively low efficiency (about 30%), which is probably due to the inef-
ficient binding of SpCas9-NG to the TG PAM (Extended Data Fig. 4a). We 
next differentiated the edited HSPCs into erythroid cells in vitro and 
measured HbF levels and F-cell percentages (Extended Data Fig. 4b). All 
five strategies effectively induced HbF expression at comparable levels.
To assess editing specificity, we next examined guide-RNA- 
dependent off-target mutations induced by the five editing strate-
gies. For sgRNAs targeting the BCL11A-binding motif in the HBG1/2 
promoters (msgHBG-boxB and sgHBG), we incorporated an additional 
bioinformatic algorithm, COSMID46,47, alongside Cas-OFFinder and 
Digenome-seq to identify off-target mutations. COSMID identified 
22 additional candidate sites, which brought the total of potential 
off-target sites to 145: 87 using Cas-OFFinder, 36 using Digenome-seq 
and 22 using COSMID (Supplementary Table 2). Among these, 
hA3A-BE3 induced substantial off-target editing at 4 sites (editing 
frequencies of 9.4%, 3.3%, 35.1% and 21.2%), whereas ABE8e caused 
detectable editing at 2 sites (editing frequencies of 6.6% and 9.9%, iden-
tified using Digenome-seq) (Extended Data Fig. 4c). By contrast, no 
detectable off-target editing was observed with tBE at any of these sites 
(Extended Data Fig. 4c). For the sgRNA targeting the BCL11A erythroid 
enhancer (sg1620), we evaluated 28 potential off-target sites predicted 
by Cas-OFFinder. hA3A(N57Q)-BE3 induced off-target editing at 2 
sites (10.0% and 4.2%), whereas ABE8e caused substantial off-target 
activity at multiple sites (67.0%, 92.0% and 82.9%) (Extended Data 
Fig. 4d). For the sgRNA targeting the CAGATA–175 motif in the HBG1/2 
promoters (sg175), we analysed two previously reported off-target

Fig. 1 | Preclinical study of tBE-mediated editing in HSPCs from healthy 
donors. a, Schematic of the BCL11A-binding motif (TGACCA) in the HBG1/2 
promoters. TSS, transcription start site. HPFH mutations are highlighted by  
the blue arrow. b, The binding of hsgHBG-MS2 and msgHBG-boxB directs tBE 
assembly. The msgHBG-boxB-recruited carboxy-terminal fragment of the TEV 
protease (TEVc) and the freely expressed amino-terminal fragment of the TEV 
protease (TEVn) forms an intact TEV protease. The intact TEV protease cleaves 
its recognition sequence between mA3CDA1 and mA3dCDI, which triggers 
editing5,6. MCP, bacteriophage MS2 coat protein; N22p, bacteriophage λ N 
peptide 1–22; nCas9, Cas9 nickase; UGI, uracil DNA glycosylase inhibitor.  
c, Editing efficiencies were calculated as the percentage of the sequencing reads 
with edited motifs, including TGADCA, TGACDA and TGADDA (where D = A/G/T). 
NT, not treated. d,e, Ratio of HbF to total haemoglobin (d) and percentage of 
F-cells (e) in tBE-edited HSPCs after differentiation. f, Engraftment efficiency 
of untreated and tBE-edited HSPCs. g, Percentage of differentiated human 
B cells, myeloid cells and T cells. h, Comparison of the editing efficiency of 
engrafted cells after transplantation to that of the input cells. i, No obvious  
off-target editing was detected at 123 potential off-target sites in tBE-mediated 
edited cells compared with untreated (NT) HSPCs. In c–i, data are the mean ± s.d. 
In c–e and i, n = 5 biologically independent CD34+ HSPC samples. In f and g, 
n = 8 and n = 9, respectively, biologically independent mice transplanted with 
untreated or tBE-mediated edited CD34+ HSPCs. In h, n = 11, 9, 10, 12, 9 and 10 
biologically independent mice at 4, 8, 12, 16, 20 and 26 weeks, respectively, 
after transplantation. In d–f, P values were calculated by one-tailed paired 
t-test. In g, the combined P value was calculated by one-tailed meta-analysis 
(Stouffer’s Z-score). NS, not significant. Schematic in b adapted from ref. 6,

show detectable off-target activity at any of these 12 sites (Extended 
Data Fig. 4e).
To evaluate guide-RNA-independent off-target editing, we used the 
co-expressing Staphylococcus aureus and Streptococcus pyogenes Cas9 
orthologues (CESSCO) assay, a published R-loop assay using S. aureus 
Cas9 nickase (nSaCas9)5 (Extended Data Fig. 5a). At the R-loop regions 
formed by nSaCas9 and the sgRNA for S. aureus, tBE did not induce 
overt guide-RNA-independent mutations, whereas hA3A-BE3 caused 
substantial off-target activity (Extended Data Fig. 5b,c).
Together, these results demonstrate that although hA3A-BE3, 
hA3A(N57Q)-BE3 and ABE8e are highly efficient for on-target editing 
and HbF induction, they also induce variable, and in some cases sub-
stantial, off-target mutations. By contrast, unlike conventional base 
editors, tBE did not exhibit detectable off-target activity (Figs. 1i and 2c 
and Extended Data Fig. 4c), thereby providing enhanced specificity. Col-
lectively, these findings establish tBE-mediated base editing as a strategy 
that combines high potency with a favourable specificity profile.
Patient and engraftment characteristics
Building on our preclinical findings (Figs. 1 and 2) and on the chemistry, 
manufacturing and control (CMC), toxicology and pharmacology data 
(Extended Data Fig. 6b–e and Supplementary Table 3), we advanced the 
tBE-mediated strategy into a phase 1 clinical trial to evaluate its safety 
and efficacy in patients with β-thalassaemia (ClinicalTrials.gov identi-
fier: NCT06024876; Extended Data Fig. 6a). Among these patients, 3 
(60%) had the β0/β0 or β0/β0-like genotype (β0/β0 in 2 (40%) and β0/β0- 
like in 1 ((20%)) and 2 patients (40%) had the β0/β+ genotype (Table 1). 
The sex distribution was three male and two female patients. All five 
patients had severe β-thalassaemia (TDT), with a median annualized 
transfused RBC unit value of 64.3 units (range, 55.8–89.5 units) at base-
line. All patients underwent a single cycle of mobilization and apheresis, 
and the median dose of CS-101 administered was 8.1 × 106 CD34+ cells 
per kg (range, 4.9 × 106 to 11.2 × 106 cells per kg) (Table 1).
Data were cut off at 17 November 2025, and the median duration 
of follow-up after CS-101 infusion was 23.0 months (range, 16.7–
25.3 months) (Fig. 3a). All five patients completed the 1-year study 
period and have been enrolled in the long-term follow-up study (Clini-
calTrials.gov identifier: NCT06685536). After busulfan myeloablative 
conditioning and CS-101 infusion, all five patients (100%) had engraft-
ment of neutrophils and platelets. The median time to neutrophil and 
platelet engraftment was 16 days (range, 14–19 days) and 25 days (range, 
13–50 days), respectively (Extended Data Fig. 6f). One patient who had 
undergone splenectomy showed a relatively faster engraftment of 
neutrophils and platelets than those with non-splenectomy (Extended 
Data Fig. 6f), a finding also observed in a recent study27. However, as this 
finding is based on only one case, it should be considered preliminary 
and requires confirmation in larger cohorts.
Transfusion independence and haemoglobin
All five patients (100%) had discontinued RBC transfusion (Fig. 3a), 
and the median time to the last transfusion was 18 days (range, −10 to 
27 days) after CS-101 infusion (the patient who had undergone sple-
nectomy received the last transfusion before the CS-101 infusion). All 
patients (100%) achieved the primary end point of 6-month transfusion 
independence, which was measured from 3 months after infusion. 
Furthermore, all patients have achieved transfusion independence 
for more than 12 months from 3 months after infusion (Fig. 3a). Across 
all five patients, the duration of transfusion-free periods was 15.0–
22.9 months, starting 60 days after the last RBC transfusion (Fig. 3a).

erythroid enhancer after in vitro erythroid differentiation of the edited  
CD34+ patient HSPCs. For tBE, the slope was 0.2632 (95% CI, 0.02332–0.5032), 
R2 = 0.4063, P = 0.0349; and for Cas9, the slope was 0.1908 (95% CI, 0.09870–
0.2828), R2 = 0.9755, P = 0.0123. In a and b, significance was assessed using 
linear regression analysis, two-tailed test, no adjustments were made for 
multiple comparisons. c, Evaluation of off-target mutations. No obvious off- 
target editing was detected at 123 potential off-target sites in tBE-mediated edited 
CD34+ HSPCs from the five study participants. Data are the mean ± s.d.; n = 5.

Nature  |  Vol 653  |  21 May 2026  |  927
Early and rapid increases in total haemoglobin and HbF levels were 
observed after CS-101 infusion. Among all five patients, the mean total 
haemoglobin concentration was 12.4 ± 1.0 g dl–1 at month 3 (Fig. 3b 
and Extended Data Fig. 6g) and remained at similar or higher levels 
until the end of the follow-up period. The mean HbF concentration was 
11.5 ± 0.9 g dl–1 at month 3 and remained at higher levels until the end 
of follow-up (Fig. 3b and Extended Data Figs. 6h and 7a) and exhibited 
pancellular distribution (≥94.7%) (Fig. 3c and Extended Data Fig. 7b). 
Increases in total haemoglobin and HbF levels were observed in every 
patient (Extended Data Fig. 7c). These increases were similar in patients 
with the β0/β0 or β0/β0-like genotype compared with patients with the β0/
β+ genotype, in adult patients compared with adolescent patients, and in 
female patients compared with male patients (Extended Data Fig. 6g,h).
Notably, three patients (patients 1, 4 and 5) with the β0/β0 or β0/β0-like 
genotype produce almost no β-globin. After achieving transfusion 
independence, they synthesized little to no adult haemoglobin (HbA), 
which resulted in HbF levels virtually approaching 100% (Extended 
Data Fig. 7c). By contrast, patients 2 and 3, with the β0/β+ genotype, 
retained partial β-globin production and continued to synthesize 
small amounts of HbA following transfusion independence; therefore, 
their HbF proportions did not reach 100% (Extended Data Fig. 7c). 
Erythropoiesis-associated laboratory values tended to stabilize at 
months 6, 12 and 18 after infusion (Extended Data Fig. 6i).
Clinical editing and off-target mutation
Base editing at the BCL11A-binding motif in the HBG1/2 promoter 
region was detectable in peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) 
of the patients at month 1 after CS-101 infusion (Fig. 4a). Among the 
five patients eligible for analysis at the primary end point, the mean 
percentage of the edited BCL11A-binding motif remained stable. That 
is, it was consistently maintained at or above 34% from month 1 until 
the end of the follow-up period (Fig. 4a). In bone marrow blood cells 
of the five patients, a mean base-editing frequency of 39.8 ± 7.2% was 
detected at month 3, and this frequency also remained stable through-
out the follow-up period (Fig. 4b). The editing frequencies at the 
BCL11A-binding motif were similar among the five patients and were 
sustained over time (Fig. 4a,b). In all five patients, minimal levels of 
small indels were detected at the on-target site in the HBG1/2 promoter 
region (Extended Data Fig. 8a–c).
In addition to C-to-T editing, levels of C-to-A and C-to-G editing 
were detected (Fig. 4c). This effect is due to the removal of the 
cytidine-deaminase-generated uracil before DNA replication or 
repair and was found to accompany C-to-T editing48–50. Therefore, we 
also investigated the base-editing spectrum. That is, the fraction of 
15 editing products at C–115 and C–114 of the BCL11A-binding motif in the 
HBG1/2 promoter region. The base-editing spectra in all five patients 
were similar and remained stable over time (Fig. 4c). To determine 
whether these variants could disrupt BCL11A binding, we performed 
electrophoretic mobility shift assays (EMSAs) using the top 10 mutated 
motif sequences, which collectively represented more than 95% of the 
edited products in patient PBMCs after infusion (Fig. 4c). The EMSA 
results showed that all ten variants efficiently disrupted BCL11A binding 
(Extended Data Fig. 8d). We also examined base-editing frequencies 
in various haematopoietic lineages, including T cells, B cells, natural 
killer (NK) cells, monocytes and neutrophils (Extended Data Fig. 5d). 
Significant base editing was observed in monocytes and neutrophils 
of all five patients starting from month 1 after the infusion of CS-101, 
and base editing was maintained over time (Fig. 4d). Editing frequen-
cies gradually increased in T cells, B cells and NK cells, which may be 
because these cells are not derived from the CD34+ stem cells and may 
not be depleted during busulfan conditioning (Fig. 4d).
Off-target editing was also evaluated in PBMCs of the five patients 
eligible for primary end-point analysis. No off-target edits were 
detected across the 145 potential sites (Fig. 4e).
α-Globin chain imbalance and apoptosis
We used high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) to analyse 
haemoglobin chains in PBMCs and bone marrow cells from all five 
patients after treatment (Extended Data Fig. 9a). The HPLC results did 
not show evidence of α-globin chain imbalance in either peripheral or 
bone marrow samples. In all patients, the α-globin chain proportion 
remained consistently around 50% (Extended Data Fig. 9b). We also 
assessed apoptosis in bone marrow erythroid cells from one treated 
patient (patient 2) and found that 37.1% of cells expressed apoptotic 
marker proteins at 24 months after treatment (Extended Data Fig. 9c).
Iron overload
Serum ferritin (SF), a complex of apoferritin and its iron core, reflects 
the iron storage capacity of the body. Clinically, SF is a simple and widely 
used indicator of systemic iron burden, and chelation therapy is rec-
ommended when SF exceeds 800 ng ml–1 (ref. 51). Five patients had 
an elevated average baseline SF level (mean, 1,800.4 ± 725.5 ng ml–1) 
(Extended Data Fig. 9d). The average SF level continued to decrease 
at month 6 (mean, 1,689.0 ± 892.4 ng ml−1) and month 12 (mean, 
1,004.9 ± 524.5 ng ml–1) after CS-101 infusion (Extended Data Fig. 9d), 
but remained higher than 800 ng ml–1.
However, SF can be influenced by inflammation, liver disease, 
tumours, haemolysis or vitamin C deficiency. Therefore, it does not 
always accurately reflect iron deposition in specific organs. Excess 
hepatic iron can cause severe complications, including cirrhosis and 
hepatocellular carcinoma. Liver iron concentration (LIC) measured

using magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) provides a reliable measure 
of hepatic iron load, with LIC ≥ 5 mg g–1 of dry weight indicating the 
need for chelation therapy51. After infusion, the mean LIC of the five 
patients assessed by T2 MRI increased from 4.9 (at baseline) to 5.2 mg g−1 
of dry weight at month 6 and then further decreased to 3.9 mg g–1 of 
dry weight at month 12 (Extended Data Fig. 9e).
Cardiac iron overload is the leading cause of mortality and morbid-
ity in thalassaemia and is assessed using cardiac MRI. A T2* value of 
<20 ms is associated with iron-induced left ventricular dysfunction 
and hypertrophy52. The mean cardiac T2* values in all five patients 
remained above 20 ms (Extended Data Fig. 9f).
At baseline, all five patients were receiving iron-chelation therapy. 
Four out of the five patients restarted chelation therapy after CS-101 
infusion, and three out of these four patients later discontinued chela-
tion therapy owing to improved iron metabolism. Nevertheless, longer 
follow-up is required to fully evaluate the long-term effects of CS-101 
on iron overload.

Discussion
Base editing has the capability to induce targeted base conversions 
without the need of DSB generation33–38, thereby circumventing poten-
tial toxicity related to DSBs28,29. In this study, a high-precision tBE was 
used to modify the binding motif of BCL11A in the HBG1/2 promoters 
of patient HSPCs with the aim of reactivating the production of HbF. 
To our knowledge, we are the first to report results from a clinical trial 
that uses base editing for the treatment of β-thalassaemia.
Following the infusion of CS-101, all five patients, including three 
with the β0/β0 or β0/β0-like genotype, discontinued transfusions within 
1 month (mean ± s.d., 16.0 ± 10.1 days) (Fig. 3a). The median times to 
neutrophil and platelet engraftment were 16 days (range, 14–19 days) 
and 25 days (range, 13–50 days), respectively (Extended Data Fig. 6f). 
Subsequently, the average HbF levels rapidly increased to 11.5 g dl–1 
by the third month after the infusion of CS-101 (Fig. 3b). For compari-
son, in patients who received HSPCs edited with Cas9 nuclease at the 
BCL11A erythroid enhancer, transfusion independence was achieved at 
a mean ± s.d. of 35.2 ± 18.5 days (32 patients), with median engraftment 
times (52 patients) of 29 days (range, 12–56 days) for neutrophils and 
44 days (range, 20–200 days) for platelets27. Similarly, in a trial using 
Cas9 nuclease editing at the HBG1/2 promoters for sickle cell disease 
(3 patients), in which granulocyte colony-stimulating factor (G-CSF) is 
typically not administered, median neutrophil and platelet engraftment 
times were 20 days (range, 18–26 days) and 29 days (range, 29–40 days), 
respectively53. Notably, by 3 months after infusion of HSPCs edited with 
Cas9 nuclease at the BCL11A erythroid enhancer (46 patients), mean 
HbF levels remained below 8 g dl–1 (ref. 27). Although the cell dose used 
in this trial (median of 8.1 × 106 CD34+ cells per kg) is nearly twice that 
used for autologous transplantation of unmanipulated HSPCs, the 
CS-101 dose is comparable to those in other gene-therapy trials12,27. 
This result suggests that the HSPC dose is unlikely to account for the 
rapid engraftment observed with CS-101. Nevertheless, in contrast to 
the larger datasets available for Cas9-nuclease-based therapies27,54, the 
current CS-101 phase 1 study included only five patients. The planned 
phase 2/3 trial will provide data from a larger cohort, which will enable 
a more comprehensive evaluation of the efficacy of CS-101.
The base-editing efficiencies at the BCL11A-binding motif in the 
HBG1/2 promoters remained stable in both PBMCs and bone marrow 
cells over the follow-up period (Fig. 4a,b). Among the five patients, 
patient 5 exhibited notably higher editing frequencies in PBMCs com-
pared with the others (Fig. 4a); this patient was the only one to have 
undergone splenectomy. However, there is no evidence to suggest that 
splenectomy causally increases HSPC genome-editing efficiency. Edit-
ing efficiency in bone marrow cells from patient 5 was only modestly 
higher than in the other patients (Fig. 4b). These data indicate that 
the elevated PBMC editing observed in this patient is probably due to 
random variation rather than a true effect of the splenectomy.
Notably, base-editing frequencies of around 30% at the BCL11A- 
binding motif in the HBG1/2 promotes (patients 1, 2 and 3; Fig. 4a,b) 
were observed to be sufficient for reactivating high levels of HbF pro-
duction (Extended Data Fig. 7c). At the single-cell level, sequencing 
of the four BCL11A-binding motifs in colonies derived from individual 
bone marrow CD34+ HSPCs showed that on average, 67.2% of colonies 
contained at least one productive edit (Extended Data Fig. 10b). The 
EMSA results demonstrated that a single base alteration at C–115 or C–114 
in the BCL11A-binding motif (TGAC–115C–114A) can completely disrupt 
the binding of BCL11A to the HBG1/2 promoter region (Extended Data 
Fig. 8d). These findings indicate that approximately 70% of CD34+ 
HSPCs in the patients’ bone marrow are capable of effectively reac-
tivating the expression of at least one copy of the HBG gene per cell. 
This observation is consistent with our preclinical results7 (Fig. 2a,b) 
and is reminiscent of naturally occurring hereditary persistence of HbF 
(HPFH) mutations in this BCL11A-binding motif, such as the C>T/A/G 
(in the HBG2 promoter) and C>T (in the HBG1 promoter) mutations at 
the –114 nucleotide position55–58.
Meanwhile, approximately one-third of CD34+ HSPCs remained 
unedited in the treated patients (Extended Data Fig. 10b). Consistent 
with this result, 37.1% of bone marrow erythroid cells from patient 2 
expressed apoptotic marker proteins (Extended Data Fig. 9c). Although 
baseline apoptotic markers were not assessed in this patient, high levels 
of apoptosis in β-thalassaemia erythroid progenitors have been previ-
ously documented59. In our study, sustained transfusion independence 
was achieved with <40% of bone marrow erythroid cells expressing 
apoptotic markers. This result suggests that reducing ineffective eryth-
ropoiesis below a specific threshold may be sufficient to produce a 
durable clinical benefit. However, the long-term impact of residual 
ineffective erythropoiesis on ferrokinetics in these patients warrants 
extended follow-up and further evaluation.
We also analysed the base-editing spectra in the study participants 
and observed that these spectra were similar among all five patients and 
remained stable throughout the follow-up period (Fig. 4c). This finding 
indicates a polyclonal profile of the edited HSCs, with no sign of domi-
nant clones. Furthermore, when we examined off-target mutations in 
the PBMCs from five patients at month 12 after the CS-101 infusion, no 
off-target editing was identified (Fig. 4e). This result demonstrates the 
high specificity of tBE-mediated editing.
The observed adverse events following CS-101 infusion were largely 
consistent with those typically associated with busulfan myeloabla-
tive conditioning. There were no incidents of myelodysplasia or other 
haematological cancers during the follow-up period.
In preclinical studies, we have previously shown that tBE-modified 
HSPCs exhibit reduced cell cycle arrest and apoptosis, along with 
enhanced proliferative capacity, compared with Cas9-edited cells7. 
Consistent with this result, CS-101 enabled early neutrophil engraft-
ment (median of 16 days, range, 14–19 days) and the resulting rapid 
haematopoietic recovery may shorten patient isolation in HSC trans-
plantation units, reduce hospitalization costs and improve utiliza-
tion of transplantation resources. Moreover, the high safety profile 
of CS-101, characterized by minimized off-target mutations, supports 
broader patient eligibility, including individuals from diverse genetic 
backgrounds. A previous study reported that a sgRNA targeting the 
BCL11A erythroid enhancer induced off-target editing at a site with 
a NGG PAM sequence common in individuals of African ancestry60. 
Such population-specific off-target sites are often missed by standard 
computational and biochemical prediction methods, which rely on 
reference genomes. By contrast, the tBE system requires colocalization 
of both the main sgRNA (msgRNA) and the helper sgRNA (hsgRNA) 
to activate base editing at on-target sites, which makes unintended 
colocalization and off-target editing highly unlikely, even in the context 
of human genetic diversity. As a result, the same CMC package and

Nature  |  Vol 653  |  21 May 2026  |  931
documentation to facilitate investigational new drug applications 
can be applied globally without population-specific customization. 
In turn, this will significantly reduce development time, regulatory 
burden and cost for international use.
Nevertheless, ex vivo HSPC editing strategies (including CS-101 and 
other Cas9-nuclease-based approaches) face financial and logistical 
challenges that need to be addressed to enable broader application. 
These include the high cost of such therapy, which is largely driven 
by the complexity of individualized manufacturing workflows. Other 
considerations are limited scalability because of labour-intensive and 
site-specific procedures and safety concerns associated with myeloa-
blative conditioning.
Our study also has several limitations, including the small patient 
cohort, single-centre design and relatively short follow-up duration. 
The planned phase 2/3 trial will be crucial for evaluating a larger and 
more genetically diverse patient population across multiple centres. 
Extended follow-up will be required to enable comprehensive analyses 
of chimerism and clonality, which will facilitate more definitive assess-
ment of long-term safety, engraftment dynamics and clinical benefit. 
Furthermore, CS-101 does not overcome the inherent challenges of 
ex vivo autologous HSC therapies. Specifically, the labour-intensive 
and resource-intensive requirements for individualized HSC collec-
tion, culture and editing, and the need for cytotoxic myeloablation. 
Ongoing advances in in vivo HSC editing61 may help to address these 
obstacles.
Taken together, the data from this clinical study suggest that a 
one-time infusion of CS-101 can lead to rapid and sustained increases 
in both total haemoglobin and HbF levels, which ultimately resulted 
in early and enduring transfusion independence.

===== Akbari2026_Nature_AncientDNA-DirectionalSelection =====

Nature  |  Vol 654  |  11 June 2026  |  419
Article
Ancient DNA reveals pervasive directional 
selection across West Eurasia
Ali Akbari1,2,3 ✉, Annabel Perry2,3, Alison R. Barton2,3, Mohammadreza Kariminejad4, 
Steven Gazal5,6,7, Zheng Li8, Yating Zeng5,9, Alissa Mittnik2,10, Nick Patterson2,3, 
Matthew Mah1,11, Xiang Zhou12, Alkes L. Price3,13,14, Eric S. Lander3,15,16, Ron Pinhasi17,18, 
Nadin Rohland1,2,3, Swapan Mallick1,2,3,11 & David Reich1,2,3,11 ✉
Ancient DNA has transformed our understanding of population history1, but its 
potential to reveal as much about human evolutionary biology has not been realized 
because of limited sample sizes and the difficulty of distinguishing sustained rises  
in allele frequency increasing fitness—directional selection—from shifts due to 
migrations, population structure, or non-adaptive purifying or stabilizing 
selection2–7. Here we present a method for detecting directional selection in ancient 
DNA time-series data that tests for consistent trends in allele frequency change over 
time, and apply it to 15,836 West Eurasians (10,016 with new data). Previous work has 
shown that classic hard sweeps driving advantageous mutations to fixation have been 
rare over the broad span of human evolution8,9. By contrast, in the past ten millennia, 
we find that many hundreds of alleles have been affected by strong directional 
selection. We also document one-standard-deviation changes on the scale of modern 
variation in combinations of alleles that today predict complex traits. This includes 
decreases in predicted body fat and schizophrenia, and increases in measures of 
cognitive performance. These effects were measured in industrialized societies,  
and it remains unclear how these relate to phenotypes that were adaptive in the  
past. We estimate selection coefficients at 9.7 million variants, enabling study of  
how Darwinian forces couple to allelic effects and shape the genetic architecture  
of complex traits.
Ancient DNA data hold extraordinary promise for revealing adapta-
tion, making it possible to track effects across time and obtain direct 
measurements of selection coefficients10,11. Rather than being trapped 
in the present and studying the scars left by selection on the genomes 
of descendants12,13, ancient DNA makes it possible to test directly 
whether frequencies of variants shifted more than could be expected 
by chance2–5. Such data also make it easier to measure selection on 
variants not of recent mutational origin (standing variation), which is 
challenging to detect using retrospective methods14. Previous ancient 
DNA selection studies in West Eurasia2–5 (Europe and its neighbours 
in the Near East) have identified dozens of alleles influenced by selec-
tion, but despite growth in reported ancient individuals to more than 
10,000 today15, the number of genome-wide significant loci reported 
in a single study grew only from 12 in the first genome scan in 2015 
(ref. 2) to 21 in 2024 (ref. 4), raising the concern that this approach might 
not deliver broad insights into human adaptation. Here we increase 
the yield of discoveries by 20-fold by adding more statistical power 
(due to a qualitatively new method and larger sample size) and reduc-
ing artefactual signals (through intensive data cleaning).
First, we increased power by testing for a consistent trend in allele 
frequency change over time. Several past studies have dealt with the 
challenge posed by population mixture by treating more recent popula-
tions as linear combinations of more ancient ones, then searching for 
alleles whose frequencies were outliers compared with what would 
be expected from this history2,3. However, changes in frequency due 
to selection are often less than what can be expected from gene flow 
and random genetic drift6, and in this context, increasing sample 
size helps little. We used a qualitatively different approach, using the 
genetic similarity of each individual to every other, and testing whether 
the date when they lived provides additional predictive power for the 
allele frequencies of their population beyond what is expected from 
the empirical population structure. Our test is simple: at each variant

Article
we asked whether hypothesizing a non-zero selection coefficient s— 
causing allele frequency to trend in the same direction over all times 
and places—predicts frequency differences across populations signifi-
cantly better than empirically measured population structure alone.
Second, we increased power through a 14-fold increase in sample 
size, driven by 10,016 ancient individuals for whom we report new 
data, which combined with previously reported data yields a dataset 
of 15,836 people spanning 18,000 years (Supplementary Tables 1–3). 
We co-analysed with 6,438 modern people, sub-sampled so that their 
countries of origin were spread evenly over West Eurasia (Extended 
Data Fig. 1a,b).
Third, we increased power by imputing diploid genotypes16, leverag-
ing known patterns of allelic correlation in a modern reference panel to 
fill in missing data in all individuals. We also carried out intensive data 
cleaning (Supplementary Section 1), filtering out single-nucleotide 
polymorphisms (SNPs) susceptible to false positives. The final dataset 
included 8,074,573 SNPs and 1,665,051 insertions or deletions (indels) 
on chromosomes 1–22.
Test of selection on single variants
For each SNP in the genome, we estimated a selection coefficient, s, 
which we found has a standard error of typically 0.17% for common 
variants (Extended Data Fig. 1c,d). In theory, a valid test for selection 
should be Z, the number of standard errors this quantity is from zero, 
and we should be able to use a normal distribution to identify scores that 
pass the standard threshold of genome-wide significance (P < 5 × 10−8). 
In practice, the median χ2 statistic (squared Z-score for the number of 
standard errors s is from zero) is inflated by 4.75-fold relative to a χ2 
distribution with 1 d.f. In human genetics studies, such inflation can 
arise due to uncorrected population structure or non-normality of the 
null distribution due to case–control sample imbalance17. Inflation like 
this is often addressed by rescaling χ2 statistics by the median inflation 
across the genome18,19. But if the null is not normally distributed, or a 
substantial fraction of the genome carries real signal18, random sites 
will not provide an appropriate neutral baseline. These concerns apply 
here: the null distribution is not normal due to frequency-dependent 
biases from imputation and the non-linear transformation of allele 
frequencies (Supplementary Section 2), and most of the genome is 
in linkage disequilibrium with sites showing evidence of directional 
selection (Extended Data Fig. 2b and Supplementary Section 3).
Instead, we calibrated our test by taking advantage of a finding 
about the connection between selection coefficients and associa-
tions to phenotypes in living people. The proportion of SNPs showing 
significant association to a phenotype in genome-wide association 
studies (GWAS) increases with our selection statistic, plateauing at 
around 5.2 times the rate for random SNPs (Fig. 1a; this analysis relies 
on 1,317,022 SNPs with a genome-wide significant association to at 
least one phenotype for 452 traits in the UK Biobank, and conditions 
on minor allele frequency (MAF) to remove bias due to signals being 
easier to detect for higher MAFs). The plateau occurs at the same place 
when we control for ‘background selection’, the loss of neutral varia-
tion linked to deleterious mutations removed by purifying selection, 
although the enrichment in GWAS variants is reduced (approximately 
2.4 times; Extended Data Fig. 3a). This is the pattern expected for a true 
threshold for genome-wide significance: if SNPs beyond this threshold 
reflect a combination of true signal and false discoveries, we would 
expect enrichment to continue beyond it. This threshold occurs at 
Z = 8.23, larger than the standard threshold (5.45) for genome-wide 
significance for a normal distribution, so we rescaled the naive score 
by this quantity to obtain an X-statistic (Z/1.51) whose significance 
threshold matches the standard threshold.
To validate our procedure, we conducted realistic forward-in-time 
simulations (Fig. 1b, Extended Data Fig. 4 and Supplementary Sec-
tion 2). The simulations specify a demographic history inspired by 
that of Europe and produce patterns of structure matching impor-
tant features of real data. The simulations include coding, functional 
non-coding and neutral regions, recapitulating varying degrees of 
background selection. The simulations also permit exploration of 
the relationship between selected alleles and traits, in particular the 
phenomenon of ‘stabilizing’ selection to remove genetic variation 
when the average genetic predictor of a trait is at a fitness optimum. 
We verified that these simulations produce a deficiency of common 
polymorphisms influencing traits, matching patterns in real data20.
Our test for directional selection is robust to every confounding fac-
tor that we considered, including population structure, background 
selection due to purifying or stabilizing selection, sampling bias and 
selection in the ancestral population but not during the time transect 
itself (Supplementary Section 2). None of these produces even a hint 
of the enrichment in GWAS signals that we observe in data. By contrast, 
in simulations with directional selection, including where we shift the 
trait optimum, leading to deterministic changes in allele frequency to 
bring the population closer to that optimum21, we observed exactly this 
signal and could use it to provide a valid calibration of genome-wide sig-
nificance (Fig. 1b, Extended Data Fig. 4 and Supplementary Section 2). 
To translate X to a posterior probability of selection π, we used a false 
discovery rate (FDR) approach (Fig. 1a,b and Supplementary Section 3). 
We fit a smooth function to the enrichment curve for GWAS signals and 
estimate that at X greater than our threshold of 5.45, π > 99%.
A second line of evidence that these signals are real comes from com-
puting the mean haplotype allele frequency (HAF) score, the sum of 
squared derived allele frequencies scaled by sample size in 200-kb win-
dows surrounding each tested variant. Previous work22,23 has shown that 
directional positive selection on derived alleles is expected to increase 
HAF scores, whereas background selection is expected to decrease it 
(Extended Data Fig. 3b and Supplementary Section 4). After comput-
ing the residual HAF score for each variant controlling for background 
selection24,25, we found that it increases with the X-statistic and begins to 
rise before 5.45, the standard threshold for genome-wide significance 
in GWAS (Fig. 1c and Extended Data Fig. 3c). Our forward-in-time simu-
lations (Supplementary Section 2) confirm that the rise in HAF score 
occurs only for directional selection, with no hint of this pattern for 
other scenarios (Fig. 1d and Extended Data Fig. 4).
We obtained a third line of evidence that we are detecting real sig-
nals of adaptation by showing that |X| > 5.45 variants are associated 
with some classes of traits much more than others. We found enrich-
ment for SNPs contributing to blood–immune–inflammatory traits 
(95% confidence interval (CI) 3.12–8.24)3,5, compared with random 
SNPs with matched characteristics defining the baseline. For mental– 
psychiatric–nervous and behavioural traits, we did not detect enrich-
ment (95% CIs of 0.90–1.08 and 0.99–1.46, respectively; Fig. 1e and 
Extended Data Fig. 5a). This cannot be explained by differences in allele 
frequencies or background selection as we controlled for these factors. 
The intensity of selection on both blood–immune–inflammatory and 
cardiometabolic traits increased significantly in the Bronze Age relative 
to the pre-farming period (Fig. 1e and Extended Data Fig. 5b), plausibly 
reflecting adaptation to new diets, larger population or living closer 
to domesticated animals26.
Hundreds of cases of directional selection
We found evidence of 479 independent loci (410 excluding the HLA 
region) with |X| > 5.45, corresponding to a π > 99% probability of selec-
tion (Fig. 2a). To produce this list, we identified the strongest signal in 
the genome and considered all SNPs in linkage disequilibrium with it 
in unrelated Western European individuals (r2 > 0.05) to potentially 
reflect the same signal. We then found the second-strongest signal 
excluding these positions, and so on until no more variants passed this 
threshold (Extended Data Fig. 2b). Visualizations of the trajectories 
for these loci (Supplementary Section 5) and summary statistics for 

Nature  |  Vol 654  |  11 June 2026  |  421
9.7 million variants (Supplementary Table 4) can be cross-referenced 
with GWAS and with their frequency trajectories at the AGES browser 
(https://reich-ages.rc.hms.harvard.edu).
The actual number of loci under selection is likely to be much larger. 
Using a threshold of |X| > 3.61 (FDR = 50%), we identified 7,689 non-HLA 
loci, implying more than 3,800 independent episodes of selection. The 
exact number of distinct signals is difficult to count: residual linkage dis-
equilibrium exists among candidate loci, which at some loci may cause 
some overestimation of signals (Supplementary Section 5), whereas 
truly selected alleles in linkage disequilibrium with nearby stronger 
alleles will be undercounted. Downsampling analyses showed that 
further increases in sample size are expected to increase the number 
of detected loci further, with people living more than 8,000 years ago 
providing the most added power (Extended Data Fig. 1d,e).
To obtain insight into the phenotypes shaped by directional selec-
tion, we took advantage of the fact that a high proportion (62%) of the 
variants with genome-wide evidence of selection are independently 
associated to a phenotype in at least one UK Biobank GWAS. However, 
biological interpretation is complicated as the allele that was the target 
of selection may differ from the tag SNP that we are using to represent 
the locus (and may even be in a neighbouring gene); because some 
alleles affect multiple phenotypes, because the relevant modern trait

of |X|: 800 simulations with directional selection (200 each for selection 
coefficients of 0.01, 0.02 or 0.03 for model 2.1, soft sweep; and 0.05 for model

enrichment comparing key cultural transitions: Mesolithic–Neolithic to

according to whether it is among the top 5% with the highest probability of a

another. Cells shaded grey denote P < 0.05.

Article
may not be measured in one of the GWAS that we are analysing, or 
because the phenotype in modern societies may not have existed in 
the ancient societies where selection acted. The median selection mag-
nitude |s| at the tag SNPs for non-HLA loci is 0.86% (range of 0.43–4.3%), 
and the median MAF is 13%. Standard errors in our estimates of |s| for 
common alleles are 0.17% (Fig. 2b and Extended Data Fig. 1c,d).
We compared our results to those of five previous scans for selection 
in Holocene West Eurasia (four based on ancient DNA)2–5,13 (Extended 
Data Table 1 and Supplementary Section 6). Of 38 unique non-HLA loci 
that met the formal threshold for genome-wide significance in at least 
one previous study, 18 passed our π > 99% threshold. The other 20 did 
not replicate, in most cases due to what appears to be incompletely 
controlled population structure driven by mixtures of populations 
with different allele frequencies before they came together and in 
a few cases due to failing quality control or fluctuating selection in 
space or time.
We present a gallery of 36 single-allele trajectories of particular 
interest (Fig. 3) as well as estimates of how their selection coefficients 
changed over time (Extended Data Fig. 6). These loci are not necessar-
ily those with the largest X scores, but are highlighted as they address 
long-standing debates. They include 27 passing the π > 99% threshold, 
5 with probable evidence of selection (57% < π < 93%) and 4 with sur-
prising negative findings.
HLA-DQB1: selection in favour of the major risk factor for coeliac 
disease
At the HLA region of chromosome 6, densely packed genes have key 
roles in microorganism recognition. rs3891176 (C>A, meaning that the 
ancestral allele is C and the newly arising mutation is A) is an excellent 
tag for HLA-DBQ1*02/DQ2 HLA-DQB1*02/DQ2, with individuals carrying 
two A alleles having a 19-fold higher susceptibility for coeliac disease 
or gluten sensitivity (Extended Data Fig. 7a,b). The A allele has a selec-
tion coefficient of s = 4.4% (π > 99%), rising from approximately 0% to 
approximately 20% in the past 4,000 years (Fig. 3a). These findings 
suggest that the pathogenic exposures that drove its rise were not a 
phenomenon only or largely of the rise of agriculture27.
ABO: positive selection for B at the expense of A
The ABO gene modifies oligosaccharides in glycoproteins on the surface 
of red blood cells; its A, B and null (O) alleles encode distinct glycopro-
tein alterations that modulate resistance and susceptibility to diverse 
pathogens28. The B allele rose from approximately 0% to approximately

Fig. 3 | Gallery of single-variant allele frequency trajectories. a–aj, Each 
panel displays the derived allele frequency trajectory (solid line) over time  
for a variant (uncorrected for structure), along with selection coefficient (s), 
selection statistic (X) and posterior probability of selection (π). The shaded 
regions represent 95% CIs around the estimated trajectory. The circles 
represent frequencies in Western hunter–gatherers (green; n = 131), early 
European farmers (orange; n = 452) and steppe pastoralists (pink; n = 293); the 
error bars indicate 95% CIs. The highlighted variants are not necessarily those 
with the strongest signals and may include negative results or variants in  
partial linkage disequilibrium. We highlight them here because of their 
biological interest and because they speak to long-standing debates. For 
panels d–f,ag,ai,aj, separate analyses are shown for transects before (blue)  
and after (red) a manually selected peak (marked by a black dashed line). In 
cases where π > 99%, the CI is shaded blue (or blue before and red after the 
split); otherwise, the shading is grey. Variants reported in other ancient DNA 
studies are marked with an asterisk.

hypothesized to have been positively selected29, but we detected nega-
tive selection (s = −0.9%, π > 99%), decreasing from approximately 50% 
to approximately 20% in the past 7,000 years (Fig. 3c). This is expected 
to have decreased baldness by 1.8% compared with the no-selection 
expectation.
TYK2: reversal of selection at a major risk factor for tuberculosis
Individuals carrying two copies of the rs34536443 G>C allele have more 
than 80% prevalence of clinically significant tuberculosis30. Previous 
work30 has found evidence of negative selection on the C allele and 
hypothesized that this allele was associated with the time tuberculosis 
became endemic in Europe. We confirmed a decrease from approxi-
mately 9% to about 3% in the past approximately 3,000 years (s = −1.9%, 
π > 99%), but also positive selection from approximately 9,000 to about 
3,000 years ago, from approximately 0% to approximately 9% (s = 2.3%, 
π > 99%; Fig. 3d). This may reflect changing endemicity of different 
pathogens over time.
HLA-DRB1: elevated multiple sclerosis risk in northern Europe is 
not due to selection on the steppe
A previous study31 discovered positive selection at the rs3135388 
G>A tag SNP for the HLA-DRB1*15:01 risk factor for multiple sclerosis. 
Because selection was already occurring in Yamnaya steppe pastoral-
ists, and Yamnaya ancestry is most common in northern European 
individuals, the authors argued that the higher risk for multiple scle-
rosis in northern European individuals was driven by Yamnaya ances-
try and selection on the steppe. We confirmed positive selection at 
this allele, rising from approximately 0% to about 16% approximately 
6,000–2,000 years ago (s = 4.2%, π > 99%; Fig. 3e). However, the pri-
mary driver of the north–south differential was not selection on the 
steppe (Supplementary Section 7). First, selection began south of the 
Caucasus mountains in people without steppe ancestry. Second, after 
Yamnaya ancestry spread west, selection was stronger in northern 
Europe at s = 11.1 ± 2.5% than in southwestern Europe at s = 6.1 ± 2.1% 
(more than 3,500 years before present (bp)); this was the main driver of 
the north–south differential. Third, we detected negative selection in 
the past approximately 2,000 years missed by previous work (s = −2.1%, 
π > 99%), plausibly reflecting new pathogen exposures.
HFE: reversal of selection at the major risk factor for 
haemochromatosis
The rs1800562 (G>A) allele predicts pathogenic iron buildup in cells in 
individuals with two copies, and we found evidence of positive selection 
from approximately 5,000–2,000 years ago, rising from approximately 
1% to about 5% (s = 2.7%, π > 99%), then dropping to approximately 4% 
today (s = −1.3%, by itself not significantly different from zero at π = 11%, 
but the decrease relative to the earlier positive selection is significant 
at P = 2.8 × 10−9; Fig. 3f). It has been hypothesized that the causal allele 
protected against Yersinia pestis (the agent of the Black Death)32, but 
this is unlikely as its frequency was decreasing by the time of the Jus-
tinianic and medieval pandemics33.
CCR5-Δ32: positive selection at an allele conferring immunity to 
HIV-1 infection
The CCR5-Δ32 allele confers complete resistance to HIV-1 infection 
in people who carry two copies34. An initial study dated the rise of 
this allele to medieval times and hypothesized that it was selected 
for resistance to the Black Death35. However, improved genetic maps 
revised its date to more than 5,000 years ago and the signal became 
non-significant36. We found that the allele was positively selected 
approximately 6,000–2,000 years ago (Fig. 3g and Extended Data 
Fig. 6g), increasing from approximately 2% to about 8% (s = 1.2%, 
π > 99%). This is too early to be explained by the medieval pandemic, 
but ancient pathogen studies have showed that Yersinia was endemic 
in West Eurasia for the past approximately 5,000 years37, resurrecting 
the possibility that it was the cause, although other pathogens are 
possible.
Selection for light skin tone at ten loci
We found nine loci with genome-wide signals of selection for light skin 
tone, one probable signal and no loci showing selection for dark skin 
tone (Fig. 3h–q).
CFTR: no evidence of selection for the major cystic fibrosis risk 
allele ΔF508
The major risk allele for this recessive disease in European individuals 
has been hypothesized to be an example of heterozygote advantage due 
to conferring resistance to cholera in carriers38, keeping its frequency 
substantial despite its strong deleterious effects in homozygotes. How-
ever, we found no evidence of directional selection over the time frame 
that cholera was probably endemic in West Eurasia (π < 1%; Fig. 3r), 
with the earliest direct observation approximately 6,550 years bp in 
Croatia and the earliest imputed one approximately 15,411 years bp 
in Anatolia. Another explanation is needed for the persistence of the 
allele, plausibly by balancing selection.
Fifteen other selection discoveries are highlighted (Fig. 3s–ag). Most 
are highly significant at π > 99%: TSBP1 (coeliac disease, s = 5.6%); a 
second allele at HLA-DQB1 (coeliac disease, s = 1.0%); HLA-DRB1 
(rheumatoid arthritis, s = −0.9%); GYPA (increases MNS blood group 
N, s = −0.9%); DUOX2 (increases ferritin level, s = 1.3%); SLC22A4 (Crohn’s 
disease, s = 1.8%); TLR1 (leprosy resistance, s = 2.0%); CYP1A2 (decreases 
blood pressure, s = 1.0%); NADSYN1/DHCR7 (increases vitamin D levels, 
s = 0.9%); ADH1B (lower risk for alcoholism, s = 2.3%); and ABCG2 (gout, 
s = 0.9%). Four more signals are probable: APOE (hyperlipidaemia, 
s = 0.9%, π = 87%); GCKR (hyperlipidaemia or gout, s = 0.5%, π = 84%); 
SERPINA1 (α1-antitrypsin deficiency, s = 1.6%, π = 57%); and a second 
locus at SERPINA1 (α1-antitrypsin deficiency), which shows positive 
selection from approximately 7,000 to 2,500 years ago (s = 2.5%, 
π = 90%) followed by a reversal after approximately 2,500 years ago 
(s = −2.3%, π = 93%).
Figure 3ah–aj highlights null signals at loci previously hypothesized 
to be selected: PTPN22 (hypothyroidism), a second allele at HFE (haemo-
chromatosis) and IL23R (Crohn’s disease).
Tests for selection on complex traits
We searched for evidence that groups of alleles with similar influence 
on traits today trended in the same direction in the past, as expected if a 
phenotype with a similar genetic underpinning was the target of selec-
tion. We leveraged 563 GWAS results in people of European ancestry: 
452 mostly quantitative traits in the UK Biobank, and 111 curated traits 
from studies especially of common disease (Supplementary Table 5). 
How phenotypes manifest today may be very different from how they 
manifested in past populations living in different environments with 
different lifestyles, so any signals discovered by this approach should 
not be interpreted as evidence for selection on the exact phenotype 
being tested.
We used three statistics to test for coordinated selection on alleles 
affecting the same trait (we excluded variants at the HLA locus as we 
were interested in polygenic signals, not those dominated by large 
effect variants). First, we computed a polygenic score (PGS) for each 
GWAS: a linear combination of allelic values, weighted by estimated 
effect size. We evaluated whether the change in PGS over time γ (scaled 
so one unit corresponds to a standard deviation change over ten mil-
lennia) is more than could be expected by genetic drift or population 
structure, using the genetic relatedness matrix to control for these 
effects as in our single-allele tests. To test whether the observed devia-
tion is significant, we repeated the test 100 times with randomly flipped 
signs of GWAS effects, to correct for residual inflation. Second, we 
repeated the procedure without using magnitudes of GWAS effects, 

Nature  |  Vol 654  |  11 June 2026  |  425
and instead only the sign, generating a statistic γsign that may be less 
affected by concerns about transferability of PGS across groups3,39. 
Third, we performed a SNP-by-SNP comparison for each trait, using 
cross-trait linkage disequilibrium score regression (LDSC)40 to esti-
mate genetic correlation (rs) between selection coefficients (s) and 
trait effect sizes. We computed a standard error from a block jackknife 
to test whether this correlation is different from zero, accounting for 
non-independence of SNPs. We found high Pearson’s correlation for 
all three tests (0.78–0.92; Extended Data Fig. 8).
For 31 traits, we repeated analyses using effect sizes measured in East 
Asian GWAS. Population structure in East Asian individuals is uncor-
related to that in ancient West Eurasian individuals, so any validation 
by this test must reflect a real signal of selection3,39.
For 34 traits, we repeated analyses using effect sizes estimated 
from family-based GWAS. This analysis distinguished ‘direct effects’ 
of genetic variants on the biology of the individual being examined, 
from ‘indirect effects’ that affect the trait by modulating the behaviour 
of family members and thereby changing the environment of the indi-
vidual. Despite much reduced sample sizes, standard errors are small 
enough for some traits to validate our findings.
Multiple lines of evidence point to the robustness of our tests of 
polygenic selection to the potential confounding factor of popula-
tion structure. First, we carried out ordinary linear regression search-
ing for evidence of a change in the polygenic predictor of height over 
time and found a significant signal without correction for structure 
(P = 7 × 10−159)41,42, which disappeared after our correction: (γ, P = 0.21). 
Second, we observed strong Pearson’s correlations between Z-scores 
for European and East Asian GWAS of 0.43 (γ), 0.75 (γsign) and 0.81 
(rs; Extended Data Fig. 12a); population structure is uncorrelated for 
European and East Asian individuals, and thus structure artefacts can-
not explain this signal. A third line of evidence comes from positive cor-
relations between Z-scores from population GWAS and direct genetic 
effect GWAS from family-based studies: 0.51 (γ), 0.51 (γsign) and 0.76 
(rs; Extended Data Fig. 12b). Fourth, we did not observe false positives 
in realistic simulations of European structure without directional selec-
tion (Supplementary Section 2).
Directional selection on complex traits
In total, 44 of 563 GWAS showed significant signals across all three tests 
after correction for the number of GWAS examined (P < 8.9 × 10−5), 
and 100 GWAS showed signals in at least one test after correction for 
multiple hypothesis testing (P < 3.0 × 10−5; Supplementary Table 5). We 
focused on 12 traits of particular interest with significant signals from 
all three tests (Fig. 4 and Extended Data Fig. 9).
One of the strongest signals is an increase over time in the PGS for 
light skin pigmentation (γ = 1.80 ± 0.10 standard deviations increase 
in mean PGS in ten millennia; P = 5.7 × 10−74; Fig. 4 and Extended Data 
Fig. 9). This plausibly reflects selection for increased synthesis of vita-
min D in regions of low sunlight in farmers with little of it in their diets. 
Most of the phenotypic shift is driven by a handful of loci43 (52% due to 
SLC45A2 alone, and 75% to the top 10 loci); however, the signal is highly 
polygenic: we need to drop the top 79 loci before the signal disappears 
(Extended Data Fig. 10). A model in which selection for pigmentation 
impacted all variants in proportion to their effect size fits (P = 0.11; 
Extended Data Fig. 11).
Type 2 diabetes risk factors give compelling signals of negative 
selection. Thus, we observed negative selection on combinations of 
alleles that today increase body fat percentage (γ = −1.04 ± 0.13), waist 
circumference (γ = −0.91 ± 0.13) and waist-to-hip ratio (γ = −0.76 ± 0.13; 
Fig. 4), which could be consistent with the ‘Thrifty Genes’ hypothesis44 
that a genetic adaptation to store fat to promote survival during periods 
of scarcity in hunter–gatherers became deleterious after the transi-
tion to food production. Skeletal evidence for nutritional stresses in 
early European farmers45–48 could be viewed as in tension with this 
hypothesis, but is consistent with it if a farming lifestyle was associ-
ated with famines that occurred on too-infrequent a timescale to be 
effectively buffered by higher fat. The signal of selection against the 
polygenic predictor of type 2 diabetes itself just misses the multiple 
hypothesis-testing corrected threshold of |Z| > 3.92 (γ = –0.43 ± 0.12 
(Z = –3.59), γsign = −0.48 ± 0.12 (Z = –3.85), rs = −0.14 ± 0.04 (Z = –3.59)).
We detected negative polygenic selection against alleles associated 
today with psychoses such as bipolar disorder (γ = −0.63 ± 0.13) and 
schizophrenia (γ = −0.74 ± 0.12; Fig. 4). These results might at first seem 
surprising given our finding that significant signals of selection are not 
enriched for variants with genome-wide significant evidence of modu-
lating psychiatric traits in GWAS (Fig. 1d). However, for variants with 
sub-genome-wide significant signals in GWAS, we did observe heritabil-
ity enrichment49 (Extended Data Fig. 5a). Brain traits have qualitatively 
different genetic architectures from blood–immune–inflammatory 
traits, with a higher proportion of sites modulating them and smaller 
effect sizes on average per allele50. Thus, our observations probably 
reflect a situation in which alleles with strong enough selection coef-
ficients to be significant are concentrated in immune traits, whereas 
selection on brain traits is so polygenic that most individual contribut-
ing alleles have too-small selection coefficients to be detected. Indeed, 
directional selection against the genetic predictors today predispos-
ing to psychosis is extraordinarily polygenic: we have to drop 584 loci 
for bipolar disorder and 825 loci for schizophrenia for the signals to 
disappear (Extended Data Fig. 10).
We observed signals of selection for combinations of alleles that are 
today associated with healthy lifestyles into old age. This includes selec-
tion against alleles today associated with smoking (γ = −0.68 ± 0.13), 
against alleles contributing to overall health decline (γ = −0.83 ± 0.13) 
and for alleles that promote faster walking pace (γ = 0.87 ± 0.13).
We finally observed signals of selection for combinations of alleles 
that today are associated with three correlated behavioural traits: 
scores on intelligence tests (increasing γ = 0.74 ± 0.12), household 
income (increasing γ = 1.12 ± 0.12) and years of schooling (increasing 
γ = 0.63 ± 0.13). These signals are all highly polygenic, and we have 
to drop 449–1,056 loci for the signals to become non-significant 
(Extended Data Fig. 10). The signals are largely driven by selection 
before approximately 2,000 years bp, after which γ tends towards zero 
(Extended Data Fig. 9).
We were concerned that the evidence of polygenic selection for 
behavioural traits might be an artefact of uncorrected population 
structure in West Eurasia, despite our simulations showing that our 
methodology corrects for such structure. To explore this, we first 
focused on years of schooling, for which available GWAS were larg-
est. We tested for a correlation of East Asian GWAS effect size meas-
urements to West Eurasian selection coefficients. Despite reduced 
power—driven by the smaller sample sizes of East Asian GWAS, and 
differences in linkage disequilibrium structure and genetic architecture 
across populations51–53—we replicated the signal of selection, which 
cannot be explained by imperfectly corrected West Eurasian population 
structure, which is uncorrelated to that in East Eurasia: γ (P = 7.3 × 10−5), 
γsign (P = 2.1 × 10−5) and rs (P = 3.9 × 10−12; Extended Data Fig. 12a). We also 
replicated using family-based GWAS54, which is immune to population 
structure (Extended Data Fig. 12b). Despite limited power of these 
studies due to small sample sizes, the PGS for direct effects on years 
of schooling showing nominal evidence of positive selection in West 
Eurasian individuals by all three tests: γ (P = 0.027), γsign (P = 0.016) and 
rs (P = 0.029). The score capturing indirect genetic effects—parental 
alleles not passed to offspring and thus modulating parental behaviour 
and family environment—showed a signal in one test: γ (P = 0.920), γsign 
(P = 0.945) and rs (P = 0.009).
We could not obtain insight into the phenotype that was being selected 
in the past through functional stratification of the years of schooling sig-
nal. Thus, when we compared GWAS for the cognitive and non-cognitive 
components of the years of schooling trait55, both showed significant 

426  |  Nature  |  Vol 654  |  11 June 2026
Article
signals individually, but their pairwise differences were not significant: 
all three tests were P > 0.05 (Supplementary Table 5). We also analysed 
brain volume56, which is correlated with years of schooling (LDSC genetic 
correlation (rg) = 0.26 ± 0.03) and found no signals (all P > 0.05).
There are caveats when interpreting signals of polygenic adaptation, 
especially for the three genetically correlated traits of scores on intel-
ligence tests, household income and years of schooling. These traits are 


===== Jiang2026_Nature_OTOF-GeneTherapy-Deafness =====

Multicentre gene therapy for OTOF-related 
deafness followed up to 2.5 years
Luoying Jiang1,2,3,4,5,18, Xiaoting Cheng1,2,4,18, Jun Lv1,2,4,18, Yuxin Chen1,2,4,18,  
Xiaoyun Chen1,2,3,4,5,18, Rongqun Zhai6,18, Liqin Zhang7,18, Lei Han1,2,3,4,5, Yiling Zhang8, 
Juhong Zhang9, Di Deng10, Zhicheng Huang11, Qi Cao1,4, Xin Zhang1,2,3,4,5, Daqi Wang1,2,4, 
Yizhe Wang1,2,3,4,5,12, Liheng Chen1,2,4, Sha Yu1,2,4, Luo Guo1,2,4, Bowen Zhang1,2,4,  
Hui Wang1,2,4, Yi Zhou13, Liling Dai1,2,3,4,5,14, Wei Wang1,2,3,4,5, Longlong Zhang1,2,4,  
Yanbo Yin1,4, Guiqing Cheng1,2,4, Ziyi Zhou8, Wuqing Wang1,4, Bing Chen1,4, Wei Lu6, 
Hongqun Jiang15, Zhiqiang Gao7, Dazhi Shi11 ✉, Yuanping Xiong15 ✉, Yu Zhao10 ✉,  
Wei Yuan9 ✉, Qin Wang8 ✉, Guodong Feng7 ✉, Huawei Li1,2,3,4,5 ✉, Zheng-Yi Chen16,17 ✉ & 
Yilai Shu1,2,3,4,5 ✉
Autosomal recessive deafness 9, caused by OTOF gene mutations, is characterized  
by severe-to-complete congenital deafness1. Although gene therapy has shown 
benefits in a small number of patients2–5, its safety and efficacy across broader age 
ranges and longer follow-up periods, as well as predictors of treatment outcomes, 
remain unclear. In this single-arm, multicentre trial conducted at eight centres,  
42 participants (aged 0.8–32.3 years) received adeno-associated virus (AAV) serotype 
1 carrying a human OTOF coding transgene (AAV1-hOTOF) at three vector dose 
groups, with up to 2.5-year follow-up. The primary end point was dose-limiting 
toxicity within 6 weeks. The secondary end point assessed efficacy and adverse 
events. No dose-limiting toxicities were observed. Grade 3 adverse events included 
decreased neutrophil count. Hearing was recovered in 90% of participants treated 
with AAV1-hOTOF, with gradual and stable improvement in auditory brainstem 
response threshold from greater than 97 ± 1 dB normalized hearing level at baseline  
to 54 ± 3, 51 ± 3, 50 ± 3 and 42 ± 5 dB normalized hearing level at 1, 1.5, 2 and 2.5 years, 
respectively, and behavioural audiometry improving from greater than 96 ± 3 dB 
hearing level at baseline to 37 ± 5 dB hearing level at 2.5 years. Participants aged  
0.5–18 years showed greater hearing improvement than adults. A higher number  
of present distortion product otoacoustic emissions at baseline or biallelic non- 
truncated OTOF variants was associated with better hearing recovery. Participants 
with hearing recovery demonstrated gradual improvement in speech perception. 
AAV1-hOTOF is well-tolerated and efficacious across a broader patient population, 
with sustained therapeutic benefits for up to 2.5 years. Chinese Clinical Trial  
Registry registration: ChiCTR2200063181.
Approximately 2 to 3 in 1,000 newborns worldwide are affected by 
hearing loss, with up to 60% caused by genetic mutations6,7. Autoso-
mal recessive deafness 9 (DFNB9), caused by OTOF gene mutations, 
accounts for 2–8% of hereditary hearing loss and approximately 41% 
of paediatric cases of congenital auditory neuropathy spectrum 
disorder8–13. The OTOF gene encodes otoferlin, a calcium-sensitive 
protein essential for synaptic vesicle exocytosis in cochlear inner 
hair cells. Loss-of-function mutations in this gene disrupt the trans-
mission of auditory signals14. Individuals with DFNB9 typically pre-
sent with congenital or prelingual, severe-to-complete and bilateral 
hearing loss1, substantially affecting their language development 
and cognition7. At present, no approved drugs are available for 
treatment.
We previously developed AAV1-hOTOF gene therapy15, which was 
demonstrated to be safe and efficacious in patients with DFNB9 in a 
single-arm single-centre trial2,3. AAV1-hOTOF successfully restored the 
hearing and speech perception in 11 of 12 children (aged 1.0–11.0 years) 
with a follow-up duration of 26 weeks to 1 year2–4. Subsequently, a 
further OTOF trial reported outcomes from ten participants, with 
follow-up of 6–12 months5. However, these studies on OTOF gene 
therapy had small sample sizes and short follow-up periods2,3,5. Addi-
tionally, the longitudinal pattern of hearing recovery and speech 
performance, as well as their potential contributing factors, remain 
unknown.
In this study, we conducted a single-arm, multicentre trial, sup-
porting the safety and efficacy of AAV1-hOTOF in a larger number of

Nature  |  Vol 653  |  28 May 2026  |  1171
participants with a broader age range and longer follow-up. We also 
explored potential predictors that influence the extent of hearing 
recovery and treatment outcomes (efficacy and inefficacy).
Participants
Between 19 October 2022 and 23 May 2025, we screened 3,193 partici-
pants for eligibility and enrolled 42 participants from eight trial centres 
in China (Fig. 1). All 42 participants carried OTOF mutations classi-
fied as pathogenic or likely pathogenic. Their mean age was 5.7 years 
(range: 0.8–32.3 years), and 48% (N = 20; throughout, N refers to the 
number of participants and n to the number of treated ears) were male 
(Table 1). Participants were assigned to three dose groups: one par-
ticipant received 9 × 1011 vector genomes (vg) per ear, 11 participants 
received 1.5 × 1012 vg per ear and 30 participants received 2.1 × 1012 vg 
per ear. From the 9 × 1011 and 1.5 × 1012 vg per ear groups, 12 participants 
with 6–12 months of follow-up were reported in previous studies2–4.
At baseline, all enrolled participants had complete hearing loss, 
with the arithmetic mean for the auditory brainstem response (ABR) 
threshold greater than 96 dB normalized hearing level (nHL), auditory 
steady-state response (ASSR) greater than 97 dB nHL and behavioural 
threshold greater than 96 dB hearing level (Table 1 and Supplementary 
Table 1). The follow-up duration was from 13 weeks to 2.5 years (baseline, 
N = 42; 6 weeks, N = 42; 13 weeks, N = 42; 26 weeks, N = 34; 52 weeks, N = 23; 
1.5 years, N = 13; 2 years, N = 10; 2.5 years, N = 6). The median follow-up 
duration was 52 weeks (interquartile range (IQR): 26–85 weeks).
Primary outcomes
The primary outcome was dose-limiting toxicity (DLT) within 6 weeks 
after gene therapy. Referring to the Common Terminology Criteria for 
Adverse Events (v.5.0), DLT was defined as grade 4 or higher haemato-
logic toxicity, grade 3 or higher non-haematologic toxicity or grade 2 
or higher aural toxicity, judged to be reasonably related to the study 
treatment. No DLT was observed in 42 participants receiving any dose 
of AAV1-hOTOF gene therapy (0 of 42; 95% confidence interval: 0–8.4%).
Safety
No serious adverse events occurred during follow-up across the three 
dose groups (Table 2 and Supplementary Table 2). All adverse events 
were grades 1–2, except for the grade 3 event of decrease in neutrophil 
count observed in three participants. In the group receiving 1.5 × 1012 vg 
per ear, one participant experienced a grade 3 decrease in neutrophil 
counts after treatment, which has been reported2. In the group receiv-
ing 2.1 × 1012 vg per ear, two participants experienced grade 3 events 
(decreased neutrophil counts) at 1 week (0.88 × 109 l−1) and 2 weeks 
(0.83 × 109 l−1) after treatment, respectively. They had no clinical mani-
festations, and the events resolved spontaneously. The most common 
events were increased lymphocyte count observed in 19 of 42 (45%) 
participants, followed by elevated levels of cholesterol observed in 18 
of 42 (43%) participants. For ear disorders, vertigo was observed in 3 of 
42 (7%) participants. One participant showed two adverse events with
