Nature  |  Vol 656  |  13 August 2026  |  403
clade or category that also shared more than 50% of the LECA’s KOs 
assigned to this ancestry (donor’s descendant genomes; Methods). 
For comparison, we also considered more restrictive reconstructions 
by constraining the search to genomes of the assumed ancestor of the 
archaeal lineage in eukaryotes (Heimdallarchaeia)12,30. These recon-
structions, which expand KO acquisitions in the LECA with commonly 
co-occurring KOs in extant organisms of that donor clade, provide an 
indirect approximation for the core set of metabolic pathways pos-
sibly present in the ancestral donors (Fig. 4 and Extended Data Figs. 4 
and 5; data available in Zenodo repository24). Note that this recon-
struction was different from standard ancestral state reconstruction, 
which necessitates higher resolution on the donor’s taxonomic affili-
ation and the underlying species phylogeny. Instead, our approach 
relied on the assumption that KO co-occurrence patterns commonly 
observed in extant genomes of one phylum were likely to be in place in 
an unknown ancestor of that phylum, which transferred certain func-
tions to the LECA. The inferred pathways discussed here maintained 
their overall tendency when we altered the database composition with 
an iterative resampling approach, penalizing overrepresented taxa 
(Supplementary Methods).
Considered together, these observations depict a complex scenario 
involving a series of HGT waves from a diverse set of donors into the 
protoeukaryotic lineage, as proposed by the premitochondrial sym-
bioses hypothesis6. The inferred taxonomic identity, metabolic fea-
tures and order of acquisition support previous ideas of syntrophic 
origins within microbial-rich environments such as microbial mats31 
(Fig. 3b) but also provide further insights. For instance, whereas the 
hydrogen–sulfur-transfer-based syntrophy (HS syntrophy) hypothesis 
positioned Deltaproteobacteria (which includes Myxococcota) as the 
eukaryogenesis host5, our analysis suggests that Planctomycetota is 
an earlier contributor. This, together with the cellular complexity of 
this group32, including the remarkable potential for cell engulfment 
observed in one genus33,34, may lend some support to Planctomycetota 
as an alternative bacterial host, as previously debated35–37. Neverthe-
less, our data do not provide information on the host or endosymbiotic 
nature of any partner.
With respect to potential metabolic interactions, although the 
incomplete and indirect nature of our reconstructions made it diffi-
cult to draw specific conclusions, some of our findings were in contrast 
to other proposed models. For instance, our results indicate that the 
Asgard ancestor may not have a methanogenic nature, as proposed in 
various early models5,31,38, as none of the Asgard archaeal genomes that 
resembled the LECA footprint had a complete methyl-coenzyme M 
reductase (MCR) and coenzyme M methyltransferase (MtbA) (Supple-
mentary Discussion). There was support for fermentative metabolism 
(with 0.63 prevalence for ethanol fermentation and 0.67 for butyrate 
fermentation in the archaeal ancestor; Extended Data Fig. 4) as sug-
gested in the reverse-flow model39. Our inference moderately supports 
a potentially photosynthetic mitochondrial ancestor (with prevalence 
values for the different photosynthetic systems ranging from 0.34 
to 0.51), as proposed previously5,40,41. Sulfate reduction was inferred, 
with support for the Myxococcota donor as proposed in the revised 
HS syntrophy hypothesis5 (0.44 prevalence for sulfate reduction) but 
also for the Planctomycetota donor (0.5 prevalence), suggesting that 
the model could be adjusted to this alternative host to accommodate 
our relative timing observations. Both cultured Asgard archaea grow 
in syntrophy with sulfate-reducing Desulfobacterota partners42,43, and 
sulfur-based interactions have been proposed between extant Asgard 
archaea and Planctomycetota in mangrove sediments44, suggesting 
that this type of syntrophy can be established between Asgard archaea 
and diverse bacterial groups. We also observed a weak potential for 
ammonium oxidizing (anammox) metabolism in the Planctomycetota 
donor (prevalence of 0.23). Notably, reconstruction of the potential 
metabolic features present in the mitochondrial ancestor strongly 
supported the presence of aerobic respiration but not that of Fe–Fe 
Ni–Fe or Fe-only hydrogenases, as implied by the hydrogen hypothesis 
and reverse-flow models31,38,39. Nevertheless, further phylogenetic, 
genomic, ecological and biochemical research is needed to elucidate 
the nature of these early interactions.
Viruses as mediators of gene acquisition
The contribution of viruses to shaping the LECA proteome has 
been discussed in previous studies45–47. A recent analysis surveying 
viral sequences across eukaryotes identified a notable amount of 
virus-to-eukaryote transfer across the eTOL, despite the dominance of 
eukaryotic-to-virus transfers8. Our analysis provides a more systematic 
and specific assessment of potential virus-mediated HGT into the LECA, 
which we found to account for at least 4.5% of all acquisitions, after 
strict filtering to ensure timing and directionality. Most such transfers 
(74%) were mediated by Nucleocytoviricota, also known as nucleocyto-
plasmic large DNA viruses, a phylum of viruses that are known to infect 
diverse eukaryotes and include giant viruses with large genomes48. We 
explored trees that showed Nucleocytoviricota as the first sister to the 
LECA and included further prokaryotic sister groups, as their topol-
ogy suggested indirect acquisition of prokaryotic genes by the LECA 
through viral mediation. These prokaryotic sisters contained, as well as 
other prokaryotic groups, all the donor groups detected in the previous 
analysis, with the most represented donor corresponding to Asgard 
archaea (approximately 10%; Fig. 2c and Supplementary Table 8). Func-
tional analysis of viral-transferred genes indicated overabundance 
of genes related to signal transduction mechanisms, mainly kinases, 
chromatin and post-translational modification proteins (Fig. 2e). This 
observation was consistent with other findings indicating that viruses 
of this type infected protoeukaryotes45 and indicates the possibility

Discussion
Our results confirm and expand earlier results supporting sizeable 
gene flow from diverse prokaryotic ancestors preceding the LECA4,21 
and uncover a role for viruses as potential mediators of such transfers. 
Considering the distributions of phylogenetic distances to the closest 
relatives, our data support premitochondrial waves of gene acquisitions 
from additional major prokaryotic donors as the most likely scenario6,50, 
against a backdrop of continuous HGT contributions from a diver-
sity of other minor sources. We found support for at least two major 
non-proteobacterial bacterial donors transferring substantial amounts 
of genes: Planctomycetota and Myxococcota. Transfers from these 
donors have been identified in earlier studies4,18,21, including small-scale 
detailed ones such as the acquisition of some steroid biosynthesis 
enzymes from Myxococcota51,52. Our results support the existence of 
sizable genetic exchange with these alternative donors, suggesting 
stable interactions beyond those between the Asgard archaeal ances-
tor and the alphaproteobacterial ancestor of mitochondria. Although 
different forms of interactions beyond symbiosis could explain this 
genetic exchange, our results support the expansion of current eukar-
yogenesis models involving a single interaction between an Asgard 
archaeal ancestor and the alphaproteobacterial ancestor of mitochon-
dria to cover further interactions with other relevant partners. Models 
involving interactions with other bacterial partners could be refined in 
light of the potential donors identified here5,6. Our results also uncover 
a role of viruses, particularly Nucleocytoviricota, as mediators of some 
of these prokaryotic gene transfers. This finding can be considered in 
the light of previously proposed roles of viruses during eukaryogenesis 
(Supplementary Discussion).
Our relative timing estimates suggest early interactions between an 
Asgard archaea and a Planctomycete (bacterium), followed by later 
contributions of Myxococcota and acquisition of the mitochondrion. 
Thus, the prokaryotic-to-eukaryotic transition was probably a gradual 
and complex process, with a constant flow of HGT from diverse sources. 
Although our data do not provide information on potential types of 
interaction (endosymbiotic, ectosymbiotic, parasitic, and so on), the 
inferred metabolic capabilities of the detected donors could be used 
in comparisons with existing metabolic scenarios for eukaryogenesis 
to either adjust these scenarios or propose new ones. Microorganisms 
are known to form complex communities such as microbial mats or 
complex biofilms53, of which viruses also form active part, and it is rea-
sonable to consider that the ancestors of the LECA lived in such complex 
environments. Successive, perhaps overlapping, long-term interactions 
with different microorganisms and the mediation of viruses would 
have led to extensive gene transfer, progressively shaping the chimeric 
proteome of the LECA.
Taken together, our results challenge models depicting eukaryo-
genesis as a one-off event resulting from a binary symbiotic interac-
tion and support a paradigm shift towards more gradual scenarios in 
which the prokaryotic-to-eukaryotic transition was achieved through 
serial interactions with a diverse range of bacterial partners, leading 
to substantial gene transfer, with viruses playing a part as mediators.

Methods
Data retrieval, curation and proteome database reconstruction
eTOLDB construction. To guide dataset selection and LECA recon-
struction, we used the phylogenetic backbone of the eTOL and the 
nine eukaryotic supergroups classification depicted in Supplementary 
Fig. 1a. This represented a consensus between recent eTOL reconstruc-
tions54–58 and an operational definition of two stems and nine super-
groups that largely reflected previous classifications59. Further details 
are provided in the Supplementary Methods.
We manually selected and retrieved 276 eukaryotic proteomes 
(Supplementary Table 1; see Zenodo repository24 and Supplementary 
Information) downloaded from NCBI60, EukProt61, P10K62,UniProt63, 
Ensembl64 and SGD65, aiming to cover all supergroups represented 
in our reference phylogenetic backbone (Supplementary Fig. 1). We 
aimed for a balanced representation of divisions and prioritized the 
most complete and highest-quality genomes for each division on the 
basis of information available in the hosting databases. For each pro-
teome, we kept only the longest isoform per gene, as indicated in the 
GFF files or FASTA headers. The isoform-free proteomes were then 
analysed for completeness using BUSCO v.5.5 (ref. 66) with default 
parameters and eukaryotic_odb10, and we extracted basic statistics 
using seqkit stats v.2.8.2 (ref. 67). To assess for the presence of uncer-
tain amino acids (Xs) and low-complexity regions in the proteomes, we 
ran SEG68 with default parameters. We used an in-house script (‘Code 
availability’) to trim the low-complexity ends of the proteins and cal-
culated the coverage of low-complexity regions for each protein. If 
the coverage of low-complexity regions accounted for more than 25% 
of a protein, we excluded that protein from downstream analyses. 
Moreover, we calculated the proportion of low-complexity proteins 
in the whole proteome as a measure of the quality of the protein set 
and also discarded proteins that were larger than 10,000 amino acids 
or smaller than 50 amino acids. This resulted in a set of clean pro-
teomes. To remove redundancy and recent duplications (with respect 
to eukaryotic evolution), we clustered each proteome individually 
using MMseqs v.2 (ref. 69) with strict thresholds: minimum amino 
acid identity percentage of 80%, minimum target coverage of 0.5 and 
coverage mode 2 (–min-seq-id 0.8 -c 0.5–cov-mode 2). The repre-
sentative sequences of the clusters constituted the final proteome. 
To assess the effects of the cleaning and clustering steps, we again 
ran BUSCO v.5.5 and seqkit stats v.2.8.2. We discarded proteomes 
that showed a combined reduction in number of proteins greater 
than 50%, those with a proportion of low-complexity proteins greater 
than 50% and those with BUSCO completeness reduction greater than 
30%. This resulted in exclusion of 30 proteomes, with 256 proteomes 
remaining.
We built three eTOL datasets of 100 proteomes each, which we 
named eTOLDBA, eTOLDBB and eTOLDBC. For this, we first classi-
fied the 256 proteomes on the basis of taxonomy and distributed them 
among 9 different supergroups and 28 divisions (Supplementary 
Fig. 1). When fewer than four species were available for a division, 
we included all in the three eTOLDB databases. For the remaining 
divisions, we selected different, partially overlapping species sets 
for each eTOLDB. eTOLDB

Base editing reveals an essential role for 
NANOG in human embryogenesis
Oliver J. Bower1,2, Ana E. R. Orsi1, Riley McMahon1, Desislava Staneva1, Josephine R. Blagrove1, 
Kashish Singh3, Claire S. Simon1, Afshan McCarthy2, Patricia Garcia4, Valerie Shaikly4, 
Mohamed Taranissi4, Martin Wilding5, Paul Serhal6, Rabi A. Odia6, Mina Vasilic6, 
Meenakshi Choudhary7, Athanasios Papathanasiou8, Kay Elder8, Phil Snell8, Leila Christie8, 
Mandana Arbab9,10,11,12, David R. Liu11,12,13, Mary Herbert7,14, Katarina Harasimov1,15 ✉ & 
Kathy K. Niakan1,2,16,17 ✉
Understanding how the first cell lineages in human development are specified and 
maintained has fundamental importance and clinical implications for regenerative 
medicine, infertility and pregnancy loss. Although mouse models have provided 
valuable insights into transcription factors regulating early development, translating 
these findings to human embryos has been limited by ethical, technical and biological 
constraints. Functional studies of transcription factors in human embryos have been 
hindered by nuclease-based genome editing approaches that induce genotoxicity1–3. 
Here, to overcome this, we applied ABE8e adenine base editing4,5 to precisely target an 
exon splice donor site, resulting in a splicing defect and functional knockout of the 
developmental regulator NANOG in human embryos. This approach did not trigger 
genotoxicity and showed limited off-target editing. Loss of NANOG disrupts pluripotent 
epiblast specification and instead cells differentiate towards a primitive endoderm 
(yolk sac) or trophectoderm (placental) transcriptional programme. Retention of 
primitive endoderm differentiation in NANOG-edited human embryos reveals a 
functional compensation that is distinct from mouse, underscoring the importance of 
directly investigating human development. Our findings demonstrate an essential role 
for NANOG in human pluripotency and epiblast specification and highlight the utility of 
base editing for functional interrogation of human development.
The genetic regulation of lineage specification in the human embryo 
remains poorly understood, particularly the mechanisms by which key 
transcription factors establish and maintain the pluripotent embryonic 
epiblast. The homeodomain transcription factor NANOG is required for 
epiblast development in the mouse6,7. Moreover, NANOG contributes 
to stabilizing a pluripotency gene regulatory network that is required 
to maintain mouse and human embryonic stem cells (ESCs) in an undif-
ferentiated state8,9. In human preimplantation embryos, NANOG expres-
sion is specifically restricted to the epiblast, the precursor population of 
the embryo proper, prior to the restriction of other epiblast-associated 
factors, such as OCT4 and SOX210–12. The functional role of NANOG 
and the mechanisms by which it regulates epiblast specification and 
pluripotency maintenance in human embryos remain unclear partly 
owing to technical challenges associated with genetic perturbation 
in human embryos.
The use of CRISPR–Cas9 mutagenesis can trigger genomic instability, 
chromosomal rearrangements and unintended mutations at on-target 
sites due to error-prone resolution of DNA double-strand breaks1–3. 
Human zygotes are particularly vulnerable to these adverse effects 
due to inefficient DNA double-strand break repair, leading to high 
rates of karyotypic abnormalities after editing1,3,13. These limitations 
hinder the use of the originally described method of CRISPR–spCas9 
for studying human embryogenesis and pose a significant barrier to 
its clinical translation.
NANOG mutant hESCs lose stem cell identity
To overcome genotoxicity we used base editing, a precision genome 
editing technology that uses a deaminase enzyme fused to a Cas9 nick-
ase protein to modify individual bases in the DNA of living cells without

base editor variant ABE8e for its enhanced catalytic activity following 
phage-assisted continuous evolution5. We designed a single guide RNA 
(sgRNA) to target the splice donor sites of NANOG (Fig. 1a), reasoning 
that it would induce a splicing defect leading to intron retention or 
exon skipping, ultimately producing mutant proteins15. To minimize 
the use of valuable human embryos, sgRNAs targeting NANOG were 
first screened in human ESCs (hESCs)16 (Extended Data Fig. 1a), where

Fig. 1 | Loss of NANOG protein expression in human embryos following 
adenine base editing. a, Left, schematic of the human NANOG gene locus (top) 
and guide RNA (gRNA) (underlined in blue) located at the exon 1–intron 1 
boundary. The NGG PAM sequence, target adenine and bystander adenine are 
highlighted. The most frequent sequence observed following ABE8e base 
editing results in a mutation at the splice donor site and a bystander allele. Right, 
schematic of base editing strategy in human embryos. Human oocytes were 
fertilized by ICSI. ABE8e and NANOG-targeting sgRNA were co-delivered using a 
microinjection needle. Embryos were cultured until the blastocyst stage (6 days 
post-fertilization). Embryos were processed for: (1) immunofluorescence 
analysis as blastocysts; (2) scRNA-seq following laser dissection to enrich  
for a polar region comprising epiblast and primitive endoderm cells of the  
ICM and polar trophectoderm; (3) mural trophectoderm further dissected  
for genotyping and low-pass whole-genome sequencing (WGS) for karyotype 
analysis. Epi, epiblast; ICM, inner cell mass; PrE, primitive endoderm;  
TE, trophectoderm. b, Bright-field images of the ICSI procedure. A microinjection 
needle containing sperm and base editing reagents was inserted into the oocyte; 
the oolemma was ruptured by gentle aspiration and the sperm and base editing 
reagents were deposited into the ooplasm. Black arrowheads indicate sperm 
position. c, Time-lapse microscopy of NANOG sgRNA ABE8e-edited embryos 
developing to blastocyst stage (n = 7 blastocysts for immunofluorescence, n = 12 
blastocysts for single-cell collection). d, Immunofluorescence analysis of wild-
type (WT) and NANOG-edited human blastocysts for DNA, GATA3, GATA4 and 
NANOG. e–i, Quantification of total cell number (e), trophectoderm (f), ICM (g), 
epiblast (h) and primitive ectoderm (i) in wild-type (n = 8) and NANOG-edited 
(n = 7) human blastocysts, analysed in two independent experiments.  
Dots represent individual embryos; error bars show mean ± s.e.m. Two-tailed 
Welch’s t-test. j, Proportions of each inner cell lineage in wild-type and NANOG-
edited human blastocysts. Letters in bars indicate embryos shown in d. Scale 
bars, 50 µm.

its function remains incompletely characterized and knockdown or 
knockout phenotypes are variable11,17–21.
We selected an sgRNA targeting the splice donor site of exon 1 
(Fig. 1a), converting the canonical -GT splice donor motif to -GC with 
77.6% efficiency following transient transfection (Extended Data 
Fig. 1b,c). We targeted the AAVS1 safe harbour locus as a control for 
delivery and activity and generated AAVS1-edited naive hESCs that 
represent an earlier epiblast-like state20 (Extended Data Fig. 1d). Despite 
multiple replicates and methods, NANOG editing in naive hESCs was 
inefficient and colonies were rapidly lost (Extended Data Fig. 1d,e). 
We therefore focused on the role of NANOG in primed hESCs that 
represent a peri- and early post-implantation epiblast-like state22, in 
which we were able to base-edit the gene and observed loss of protein 
(Extended Data Fig. 1a–e). We observed that NANOG ABE8e-edited 
cells displayed lower cell density compared with AAVS1-edited con-
trols (Extended Data Fig. 1d,f). Bulk RNA sequencing (RNA-seq) of 
NANOG- and AAVS1-base-edited hESCs showed clustering with previ-
ously published primed hESCs and away from naive hESCs, indicating 
that transcriptome-wide, they retained their expected stem cell state 
(Extended Data Fig. 2a,b). NANOG-edited cells showed significant 
reduction in NANOG, PRDM14, DPPA2, DPPA5, DNMT3B, SUSD2 and 
ZSCAN2, indicating a loss of pluripotency marker expression (Extended 
Data Fig. 2c,d and Supplementary Table 1). NANOG transcript may 
be downregulated owing to nonsense-mediated decay and/or dif-
ferentiation of hESCs triggered by destabilization of pluripotency 
following NANOG perturbation. NANOG-edited cells upregulated 
neuroectoderm-associated molecular markers, including increased 
expression of LRNN3, ZEB2, PAX6, PAX9, OTX2, SOX2 and SOX9 (Extended 
Data Fig. 2c–e and Supplementary Table 1).
We next explored whether we could propagate NANOG-null mutant 
hESC lines. We performed single-cell colony cloning23 of NANOG- and 
AAVS1-base edited hESCs and cultured the resulting clones for 14 days 
(Extended Data Fig. 2f–h). After 14 days, 95.8% of AAVS1-edited clones 
formed viable, stable colonies, whereas only 41.7% of NANOG-edited 
clones formed colonies at day 7, with none persisting by day 14 
(Extended Data Fig. 2h). Remaining NANOG-edited cells were sparse, 
highly differentiated and larger in size compared with AAVS1-edited 
controls (Extended Data Fig. 2g). Altogether, this demonstrates that 
adenine base editing of a splice donor site is an effective strategy for 
disrupting gene function. This also shows that NANOG is essential for 
maintaining pluripotency and self-renewal in hESCs, highlighting a 
critical role in hESC maintenance. The phenotype that we observed in 
hESCs contrasts with Nanog-null mouse ESCs that remain pluripotent, 
undergo indefinite self-renewal, express pluripotency-associated fac-
tors Oct4, Sox2, Dppa3, Klf4, Esrrb and Zfp42, and chimerically contrib-
ute to all somatic lineages and the germline24,25, which may reflect stem 
cell state, culture condition or species differences. The role of NANOG 
in hESCs is reminiscent of that reported in primed mouse epiblast 
stem cells, where it functions to inhibit somatic cell lineages, including 
neuroectoderm differentiation26, consistent with previous studies of 
hESCs in equivalent primed culture conditions18. G-band karyotyp-
ing showed that the NANOG-edited hESCs are karyotypically normal 
(Extended Data Fig. 2i) indicating that base editing does not lead to 
partial or whole chromosome gains or losses as previously reported 
for spCas91,3.
Reduced genotoxicity and off-target editing
We next determined the efficiency of ABE8e editing in mouse embryos. 
We designed a corresponding mouse sgRNA targeting the 3′ region 
of Nanog exon 1 (Extended Data Fig. 3a). Although this sgRNA could 
be used to optimize delivery methods in mouse embryos, the lack 
of a compatible protospacer adjacent motif (PAM) sequence at the 
exon–intron boundary prevented the targeting of the splice site to 
generate an equivalent splicing mutation. We electroporated mouse 
zygotes with ABE8e mRNA and Nanog sgRNA and collected blastocysts 
at 4.5 days post-fertilization for DNA extraction and next generation 
target amplicon sequencing (Extended Data Fig. 3b). We observed 
no significant difference in rates of blastocyst formation between 
Nanog ABE8e-edited embryos (n = 13) and unperturbed controls (n = 7) 
(Extended Data Fig. 3c). Nanog-edited embryos exhibited high fre-
quency of detectable A•T>G•C edits at the on-target locus (Extended 
Data Fig. 3d,e). Single-nucleotide editing frequency was on average 
82.4% in individual embryos (n = 6), with biallelic editing detected in 
83% of embryos (5 out of 6). Pooled groups of 10 embryos showed a 
single-nucleotide editing frequency of 94.3% (n = 3 replicates, n = 30 
embryos) (Extended Data Fig. 3e). In keeping with previous reports, we 
observed silent bystander mutation of neighbouring bases, targeting 
a proximal adenine base pair because it falls within the editing window 
of ABE8e27 (Extended Data Fig. 3d). To further optimize our delivery 
approach, we performed purification of ABE8e protein for human 
embryo experiments (Extended Data Fig. 3f).
We next proceeded to target human embryos and gametes donated 
for research. We used a co-injection method that has previously been 
shown to reduce mosaicism and increase the efficiency of CRISPR–
Cas9-mediated editing28. Cryopreserved MII-stage oocytes and sperm 
were thawed. Using intracytoplasmic sperm injection (ICSI) sperm was 
co-microinjected with ABE8e mRNA or purified protein and NANOG 
sgRNA (Fig. 1a,b and Extended Data Fig. 3g). Based on our prior experi-
ence, approximately 50% of human embryos form blastocysts following 
microinjection of spCas9 protein16, consistent with typical developmen-
tal outcomes observed with in vitro fertilization in clinical settings29. 
Consistent with this frequency, 43% of NANOG-edited embryos initiated 
blastocyst formation and expansion (n = 12 out of 28 embryos) (Fig. 1c). 
We confirmed NANOG protein loss in the NANOG-edited embryos by 
immunofluorescence analysis (Fig. 1d). Co-staining and quantification 
of trophectoderm and primitive endoderm markers GATA3 and GATA4 
showed the retention of these extraembryonic lineages despite the 
loss of NANOG (Fig. 1e–j). To maximize the information gained from 
embryos used in this study, we also analysed arrested human embryos 
to evaluate editing frequency (Extended Data Fig. 3g–i). Sequencing of 
embryos that arrested prior to the blastocyst stage revealed an editing 
frequency of 62.5% (n = 10 out of 16 embryos) (Extended Data Fig. 3i). 
However, given that arrested human embryos are developmentally 
compromised and frequently carry karyotypic abnormalities30, this 
editing rate may not be representative of embryos that are capable of 
developing beyond this stage.
As blastocyst formation was not compromised, we next sought to 
investigate the consequences of NANOG loss on human preimplan-
tation embryos (Fig. 1a). Blastocyst quality was scored using clinical 
embryology grading, which showed that compared with controls 
NANOG-edited blastocysts had a poorer quality inner cell mass (ICM) 
(Fig. 2a). NANOG-edited blastocysts were laser microdissected to 
enrich for a polar region comprising epiblast, primitive endoderm 
and polar trophectoderm separated from mural trophectoderm cells 
(Fig. 1a and Extended Data Fig. 4a). Mural trophectoderm was further 
microdissected and genotyped, whereas the polar regions were dis-
sociated for subsequent single-cell RNA sequencing (scRNA-seq) and 
transcriptome analysis (Fig. 1a). We observed on-target T•A>C•G edit-
ing at the NANOG exon 1 splice donor site in 84.8% of trophectoderm 
biopsies from edited blastocysts (n = 12 embryos, 39 out of 46 biopsies) 
(Fig. 2a and Extended Data Fig. 4b). Biallelic editing was variable among 
biopsies, with homozygous mutation in 37.0% of biopsies and either 
heterozygous or mosaic mutations in 47.8% of biopsies (n = 17 and n = 22 
out of 46 biopsies, respectively) (Fig. 2a). As previously described, we 
also observed bystander complement T•A>C•G editing of a thymine 
located three base pairs upstream (5′) of the target sequence in 10 out 
of 12 (83.3%) blastocysts and 10 out of 16 (62.5%) arrested embryos 
(Fig. 2b and Extended Data Fig. 4b,c). Exclusive on-target base editing 
was observed in 2 out of 12 blastocysts (16.7%) (Fig. 2b). The bystander 

4  |  Nature  |  www.nature.com
Article
base change is a silent mutation that does not alter the amino acid 
sequence of NANOG.
To assess aneuploidy and chromosomal loss following editing, we 
performed whole-genome sequencing on trophectoderm biopsies 
(Fig. 2c and Extended Data Fig. 4d). No loss of chromosomal material 
was detected at the on-target loci or the top 5 predicted off-target sites 
in any of the biopsies analysed (n = 23 biopsies) (Fig. 2c and Extended 
Data Fig. 4d). NANOG sgRNA is predicted to edit an off-target NANOG 
pseudogene at an intergenic region, and other loci were predicted in 
silico to have low rates of editing (Fig. 2d and Extended Data Fig. 4e). The 
majority of predicted off-target sites were either in introns or intergenic 
regions with a single exonic region for a microRNA MIR485 predicted 
(Extended Data Fig. 4e). We performed next generation amplicon 
sequencing of 10 predicted off-target sites in edited blastocysts (n = 10 
embryos, 30 biopsies in total) and observed 0.15% or less edits (Fig. 2d 
and Extended Data Fig. 4e).
Previous versions of deaminase base editors have been suggested 
to lead to RNA off-target edits, particularly using the cytidine deami-
nase base editor31–34. To assess possible RNA off-target edits fol-
lowing ABE8e editing, we transfected hESCs and collected cells for 
genome-wide total RNA-seq 48 h following puromycin selection 
(Fig. 2e). We applied a previously published pipeline to determine 
edits in RNA-seq reads31 and observed A-to-G editing rate at all sites 
increased by 1.2% in ABE8e NANOG-edited cells compared with con-
trols (n = 348 loci in control, n = 415 loci in ABE8e NANOG-edited) 
(Fig. 2e). Notably, we observed similar percentages of base changes 
at other nucleotides not associated with adenine base editing conver-
sion, for example 1.6% increase of C-to-G base conversion in ABE8e 
NANOG-edited cells compared with controls (n = 91 loci in control, 
n = 145 loci in ABE8e NANOG-edited), indicative of technical DNA poly-
merase or sequencing error rates. Overall, these data demonstrate 
effective base editing in human embryos, absence of genotoxicity, low 
off-target editing and human blastocyst initiation despite the loss of 
NANOG.
NANOG is essential for epiblast specification
The retention of GATA4 in NANOG-edited human embryos was unex-
pected because Nanog-null mutant mouse embryos are defective in 
primitive endoderm specification, including the absence of GATA435,36. 
To investigate the transcriptome-wide effects of NANOG loss for human 
lineage specification, we performed scRNA-seq on dissociated polar 
regions. We isolated biopsies from 12 NANOG-edited embryos and 
single cells (359 cells) from 11 out of 12 of these because one biopsy 
lysed prior to scRNA-seq. We compared these to cells isolated from 
two AAVS1-edited controls (42 cells from 2 polar regions) (Extended 
Data Fig. 4f) and previously published reference transcriptome data-
sets37–39. Genotyping of biopsied embryos indicated homozygous 
mutant, heterozygously or mosaically edited, or wild-type alleles at 
the NANOG locus (Fig. 2a). Although these groupings informed fur-
ther analysis, trophectoderm biopsies represent only a portion of the 
embryo and may not reflect the genotype of the embryo as a whole. In 
NANOG-edited cells, scRNA-seq reads exhibited GT-to-GC mutation at 
the NANOG splice donor site, aberrant intron-retaining transcripts fol-
lowing exon 1 and absence of mapping to subsequent exons, suggesting 
a splicing defect (Extended Data Fig. 5). By contrast, in AAVS1-edited 
controls, scRNA-seq reads mapped to the 3′ untranslated region and all 
NANOG exons, with evidence of correct splicing across exonic regions 
(Extended Data Fig. 5). Reads mapping to the GAPDH locus in both the 
NANOG- and AAVS1-edited cells showed correct splicing and reads 
mapping to only exons.
We integrated these data with previously published human pre-
implantation datasets37–39 to benchmark cell populations against 
established and well-characterized embryonic lineages (Fig. 3a–c).

Fig. 2 | Minimal evidence of off-target editing and no obvious genotoxicity 
following base editing of NANOG in human embryos. a, Bar chart of observed 
A•T>G•C editing at the NANOG exon 1 splice donor site based on Sanger 
sequencing of trophectoderm biopsies from 12 NANOG-edited blastocysts 
(n = 46 biopsies). Clinical scoring of each blastocyst: 4, fully expanded 
blastocyst; 5, hatching (trophectoderm breaking through zona pellucida);  
6, fully hatched blastocyst; A, excellent (many tightly packed cells); B, good 
(several loosely grouped cells); C, fair (very few cells); and D, poor (degenerate 
and/or fragmented cells). b, Proportions of NANOG-edited blastocysts that  
had edits at the on-target base, a bystander base, both bases, or no editing.  
c, Representative whole-genome sequencing analysis of trophectoderm 
biopsies from NANOG-edited blastocyst 4, with blue arrowheads indicating the 
NANOG locus on chromosome 12 (n = 5 blastocysts, n = 23 biopsies). d, Bar chart 
of observed A•T>G•C editing at 10 in silico predicted off-target sites in pooled 
biopsies from edited blastocysts (n = 10 embryos, 30 biopsies in total). e, Jitter 
plots of observed C>G (not expected), A>G (expected) and T>C (reverse 
complement of expected) ABE8e editing across the transcriptome in NANOG-
edited hESCs as determined by bulk RNA-seq (n = 3 experimental replicates; 
numbers of RNA-seq loci is noted in the graph). Bars show mean ± s.d.

Following integration, four main cell populations were identified based 
on expression of lineage-associated molecular markers: trophecto-
derm (GATA2, GATA3 and CDX2), ICM (GATA6 and POU5F1), epiblast 
(NANOG, POU5F1 and ARGFX) and primitive endoderm (GATA6, GATA4 
and PDGFRA) (Fig. 3a–c). Cells from all three NANOG-null mutant edited 
embryos (blastocysts 4, 7 and 8) clustered predominantly with the ICM, 
trophectoderm and primitive endoderm groups, whereas cells from 
heterozygous or mosaic embryos (blastocysts 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 9, 10 and 11) 
clustered with ICM, epiblast, trophectoderm and primitive endoderm 
control cells (Fig. 3a–d).
To further understand the transcriptome-wide consequences of the 
loss of NANOG, we focused on the embryos in which trophectoderm 
biopsies suggested a homozygous NANOG mutant genotype compared 
with AAVS1 and reference control embryos. Global gene expression 
analysis revealed that compared with control cells, NANOG-edited 
embryos 4, 7 and 8 exhibited significantly reduced expression of 
epiblast-associated markers, including NANOG, LEFTY1, LEFTY2 and 
FGF4 (Figs. 3e,f and 4a and Supplementary Table 2). We found reduced 
expression of SOX2, ETV5, PRDM14, VENTX and NODAL transcripts in 
the NANOG-edited ICM cells compared with controls, although the 
difference was not statistically significant (Figs. 3e and 4a and Supple-
mentary Table 2). This suggests that epiblast specification is disrupted 
in the absence of NANOG.
Primitive endoderm in NANOG mutant human but not 
mouse
To compare the role of NANOG in mouse and human, we utilized a 
spCas9 editing strategy in mouse zygotes to target the orthologous 
3′ region of Nanog exon 1 (Extended Data Fig. 6a). We validated high 
on-target editing efficiency (Extended Data Fig. 6b), resulting in loss of 
NANOG and GATA4 protein expression in mouse embryos (Extended 
Data Figs. 6c–e). Although 4 out of 5 mouse blastocysts that we evalu-
ated following spCas9 genome editing had a normal karyotype, 1 out of

Fig. 3 | NANOG-edited human embryo cells do not specify embryonic epiblast, 
preferentially contributing to extraembryonic lineages. a, Uniform manifold 
approximation and projection (UMAP) integrated clustering of the experimental 
scRNA-seq data generated in this study with published reference datasets37–39 
(n = 359 cells from 11 blastocysts, integrated with n = 888 cells from previously 
published datasets). Cells are coloured by lineages based on marker expression in 
b and the Seurat FindNeighbors and FindClusters functions. b, UMAP feature 
plots showing relative expression of molecular markers enriched in lineages: 
epiblast (NANOG, POU5F1 and ARGFX), primitive endoderm (GATA6, GATA4 and 
PDGFRA), ICM (GATA6 and POU5F1) and trophectoderm (GATA2, GATA3 and CDX2). 
c, UMAP annotated according to experimental condition: AAVS1-edited embryos 
or NANOG-edited embryos that showed mosaic or homozygous editing based on 
trophectoderm biopsies in Fig. 2a. d, Stacked bar plots indicating the number and 
proportion of cells from each embryo experimental group mapped to each 
lineage. Published controls are shown as a comparison37–39,44. All data plotted 
underwent similar laser-dissected enrichment for the polar region and SMART-
seq based RNA-seq. e, Dot plot summarizing relative expression of lineage-
associated molecular markers across the published reference and AAVS1-edited 
embryos versus NANOG-edited homozygous embryo datasets. The percentage  
of cells and average expression are shown. f, Volcano plot with genes that are 
significantly differentially expressed between NANOG-edited homozygous 
embryos and AAVS1-edited embryos. Significantly differentially expressed genes 
(MAST test with adjusted P value < 0.05 and log2(fold change) > 1.0 (red)). 
Adjusted P values were calculated using the two-sided Bonferroni correction 
method to control for multiple tests. FC, fold change; NS, not significant.

5 was aneuploid at the on-target chromosome (Extended Data Fig. 6f), 
consistent with the approximately 20% aneuploidy that we and others 
previously reported following the use of spCas9 enzyme in embryos1. 
Off-target loci predicted in silico included intergenic and intronic 
regions (Extended Data Fig. 6g). We amplified seven in silico predicted 
off-target sites using PCR of pooled samples of three Nanog genome 
edited mouse embryos and performed next generation deep sequenc-
ing (Extended Data Fig. 6g). Four out of 7 off-target predicted sites 
lacked observed insertion deletion mutations in mouse Nanog-edited 
embryos, and 2 out of 7 sites had 10% and 2% reads with off-target 
editing (Extended Data Fig. 6g). We performed scRNA-seq on Aavs1- 
versus Nanog-edited mouse blastocysts and enriched for ICM cells 
in the polar region by laser microdissection (Extended Data Fig. 6a). 
RNA-seq reads mapping to the Nanog locus confirmed successfully 
edited cells (Extended Data Fig. 7a). Following integration with previ-
ously published scRNA-seq of mouse embryos from embryonic day 
3.5 to 4.5 (ref. 40) (Extended Data Fig. 7b,c), we observed distinct clus-
tering of Nanog-edited cells compared with Aavs1-edited controls, with 
divergent lineage marker expression (Extended Data Fig. 7d,e). Nota-
bly, Nanog-edited mouse embryos did not establish discrete epiblast 
or primitive endoderm cells, consistent with previous studies6,7,35,36. 
Instead, they clustered with ICM and trophectoderm cells (Extended 
Data Fig. 7b–d). The Nanog-edited ICM-like cells expressed Nanog, 
Pou5f1, Tbx3, Zfp42, Klf2 and Klf4, but downregulated markers such as 
Etv4, Nodal, Lefty1 and Lefty2 (Fig. 4a and Extended Data Figs. 7e and 8a 
and Supplementary Table 3). Expression of Sox2 and Fgf4 transcripts 
was reduced in the Nanog-edited ICM cells compared with controls, 
although this did not reach significance (Extended Data Figs. 7e and

Fig. 4 | Primitive endoderm differentiation occurs despite NANOG mutation 
in human, but not mouse, embryos. a–d, Comparison of scRNA-seq of human 
and mouse NANOG (or Nanog)-edited embryos versus controls. log-normalized 
expression of selected genes associated with epiblast (a), trophectoderm (b) and 
primitive endoderm (c) lineages, and RTK signalling (d). n = 930 previously 
published and AAVS1-edited control cells, n = 72 NANOG-edited cells, n = 57 Aavs1-
edited cells, n = 58 Nanog-edited cells. Box plots represent the interquartile range 
(IQR), with the median shown as a central line; whiskers (white box) extending to 
lowest and highest values within 1.5× IQR. Individual dots represent expression in 
a given cell. e, Schematic summary of the role of NANOG in human versus mouse 
preimplantation development. The loss of NANOG in human and mouse embryos 
leads to impaired epiblast specification and upregulation of trophectoderm-
associated transcripts in both species. FGF4 expression, which has been shown 
to be required for primitive endoderm specification, is disrupted in both species. 
Unlike mouse, human NANOG-base edited embryos can still differentiate 
primitive endoderm cells due to an undetermined compensatory mechanism.

Nature  |  www.nature.com  |  7
8a and Supplementary Table 3). Moreover, the Nanog-edited ICM-like 
cells significantly upregulated trophectoderm-associated markers 
Gata2, Gata3, Krt7, Cdx2, Epcam, Tfap2c, Hand1 and Aqp3, and gene 
ontology enrichment further indicated that lineage specification was 
compromised (Fig. 4b and Extended Data Figs. 8b,c and 9a and Sup-
plementary Table 3). The inability of both mouse and human embryos 
to specify the pluripotent epiblast indicates conservation of this phe-
notype between these species (Figs. 3e and 4a, Extended Data Figs. 7e 
and 8a–c and Supplementary Table 4).
The downregulation of Fgf4 expression in the mouse is likely to 
account for the failure of primitive endoderm differentiation (Fig. 4c,d 
and Extended Data Fig. 7e), because FGF4 is a key NANOG-regulated 
ligand that is essential for primitive endoderm maturation36,41,42. In 
contrast to the mouse, we note that human NANOG-edited cells dif-
ferentiated to the primitive endoderm, despite the absence of FGF4 
transcripts (Figs. 3c–f and 4c,d). This is surprising, given the role 
of receptor tyrosine kinase (RTK) signalling via the FGF–MEK–ERK 
pathway in human primitive endoderm specification43,44. We con-
firmed that NANOG-edited cells express a number of key primitive 
endoderm-associated markers such as GATA6, GATA4, PDGFRA, SOX17, 
FOXA2 and OTX2 and basement-membrane associated factors such as 
LAMA1, LAMB1, LAMC1 and COL4A1 (Fig. 4c and Extended Data Fig. 9b).
Given the lack of FGF4 transcription in NANOG-edited cells, we inves-
tigated the expression of genes encoding alternative ligands and any 
receptors associated with RTK signalling (Fig. 4d and Extended Data 
Figs. 9c). We identified expression of genes encoding FGF receptors 
(FGFR1, FGFR242, FGFR3 and FGFR4), insulin receptor genes (INSR and 
IGFR1), FGF ligands (FGF1, FGF12 and FGF18) and insulin growth factor 
ligands (IGF1). This suggests either that alternative ligands bind to FGF 
receptors or that RTK signalling is activated by alternative ligands and 
receptors that compensate for the loss of FGF4. We previously showed 
crosstalk between FGF and IGF signalling in stem cell proliferation 
and that treatment with exogenous IGF1 led to increased numbers of 
ICM cells with embryos in a diapause-like state45,46; it would therefore 
be interesting to determine whether FGF18, IGF1 or alternative RTK 
signalling components can compensate for the loss of NANOG and 
whether they may have a role in primitive endoderm specification in 
a non-perturbed context.
Overall, we show that adenine base editing is a precise and effective 
strategy for functional genetic interrogation in early human embryos, 
overcoming some of the genotoxic limitations of conventional CRISPR–
spCas9-mediated genome editing. Here we apply adenine base editing 
to disrupt NANOG, revealing a requirement for this gene in human pluri-
potency and epiblast specification (Figs. 3c–f and 4e). In contrast to 
mouse, we observed that NANOG-edited human embryos retained prim-
itive endoderm cells despite the absence of FGF4 (Fig. 4e), and our data 
suggest a number of plausible RTK signalling components that would 
be interesting to investigate in the future. The inability of Nanog-null 
mutant mouse embryos to give rise to epiblast or mature primitive 
endoderm cells, and the expression of trophectoderm-associated 
transcripts in the ICM, is consistent with previous studies6,7,35,36. This 
suggests divergence in gene function between the human and mouse, 
consistent with our previous studies indicating differences in OCT4 
function16.
Whereas base editing of exon–intron splice sites has been reported 
in growth hormone receptor in porcine cells, major histocompatibility 
complex in immune cells and to target fluorescent proteins15,47,48, to 
our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of base editing used to 
disrupt a conserved splice donor site to induce a splicing defect and 
generate a loss-of-function mutation in a developmental regulator 
across any cell type. This approach will be broadly applicable to other 
developmental regulators and a substantial improvement over other 
CRISPR–Cas9 approaches. Given that G•C>A•T substitutions underlie 
nearly half of all known pathogenic variants5, base editing approaches 
have been proposed as potential tools for disease modelling and 
therapeutic correction. Although the present study was not designed to 
assess clinical applicability of base editing in the human germline, our 
findings do demonstrate reduced aneuploidy following genome edit-
ing compared with previous Cas9-mediated approaches1–3. Although 
these observations suggest potential for base editing to investigate 
gene function in early-stage human embryos, substantial basic research 
is necessary before potential therapeutic correction. This includes 
extensive evaluation of safety, such as further assessment of off-target 
activity, genotoxicity and developmental potential in a larger cohort 
and across more genomic loci in basic research before consideration 
for clinical use in heritable genome editing. Base editor variants such 
as ABE8e-V106W5 and CBE6b-V106W49 show improved safety through 
reduced DNA and RNA off-target editing in cell and animal studies, but 
their performance in human embryos is not yet characterized. Crucially, 
clinical translation of genome editing in human embryos must not 
proceed unless there is broad societal debate and support50–52. Cau-
tion, underpinned by rigorous ethical inquiry and oversight together 
with extensive public discourse, are needed. Together, these findings 
define a previously unappreciated requirement for NANOG in human 
development and provide a generalizable strategy for precise and 
minimally disruptive genome engineering in early embryos.

===== Shu2026_Nature_CRISIS-AntiPhageDefence =====

226  |  Nature  |  Vol 657  |  3 September 2026
Article
CRISPR–Cas regulates expression of 
embedded anti-phage defence systems
Xian Shu1,7, Rui Wang1,7, Xufei Zhou2,7, Feiyue Cheng1,7, Jiayue Ma1, Zhihua Li1,3, Xin Li2, 
Tuozhan Wu2, Aici Wu1,3, Qiong Xue1, Chao Liu1,3, Huiwei Zhao1, Xifeng Cao1,3, Lin Wang4, 
Shouyue Zhang1,3, Yan Zhang2,5 ✉ & Ming Li1,3,6 ✉
Bacteria utilize diverse defence systems to protect against harmful foreign DNA such 
as bacteriophages1,2, but how these systems coordinate with each other remains 
poorly understood. Here we uncover CRISIS (CRISPR-supervised immune system),  
a widespread regulatory paradigm whereby type I CRISPR–Cas loci embed and 
transcriptionally modulate diverse innate defences. Small non-canonical CRISPR  
RNA (crRNA)-like RNAs guide the I-C CRISPR-associated complex for antiviral defence 
(Cascade) effector complex to inhibit promoters of diverse immune cassettes—
including composite multi-system clusters—enabling their basal expression for 
antiviral activity while mitigating fitness costs associated with hyperactivation, such 
as host growth impairment or exclusion of beneficial plasmids. When CRISPR–Cas is 
compromised by mutation or anti-CRISPR proteins, there is a burst in transcription  
of these embedded defence systems, leading to higher-level innate immunity at the 
expense of host fitness. Together, adaptive CRISPR–Cas systems orchestrate diverse 
innate immune systems into a layered defence network, comprising a prokaryotic 
‘immunity guard’ strategy.
Prokaryotes face relentless attacks from mobile genetic elements 
(MGEs) such as bacteriophages and plasmids. In response, they 
have evolved a diverse arsenal of defence systems that often clus-
ter in genomic defence islands1–5. However, whether and how these 
co-occurring systems coordinate to combat diverse phages remains 
poorly understood.
CRISPR and their associated Cas proteins provide prokaryotic adap-
tive immunity against MGEs6,7. Foreign DNA fragments are incorpo-
rated into CRISPR arrays as immunological memories termed spacers7. 
crRNAs derived from these arrays guide Cas proteins to complementary 
foreign sequences adjacent to protospacer adjacent motif (PAMs)8,9. 
CRISPR–Cas systems are categorized into two classes encompass-
ing 7 types and more than 40 subtypes, with type I systems being the 
most prevalent10,11. Beyond adaptive immunity, CRISPR–Cas systems 
also regulate gene expression12–15. In the type II system of Streptococ-
cus pyogenes, for example, a long trans-activating crRNA (tracrRNA) 
isoform directs Cas9 autorepression to mitigate autoimmunity16. In 
types I and V systems, solitary CRISPR repeat units (SRUs) generate 
crRNA-like RNAs (crlRNAs) that pair imperfectly with endogenous 
promoters and modulate transcription in a Cas-dependent manner, 
without triggering DNA cleavage12–15,17,18.
Although CRISPR–Cas loci frequently colocalize with other defence 
operons, the role of this arrangement has remained unclear. Here we 
show that type I-C CRISPR–Cas uses crlRNAs to transcriptionally regu-
late diverse defence systems embedded within their cas loci—systems 
that we designate as CRISIS. This supervision maintains basal immunity 
while limiting the fitness costs associated with hyperactivation, includ-
ing growth defects and plasmid loss. When CRISPR–Cas is inhibited by 
anti-CRISPR (Acr) proteins, these embedded systems are derepressed, 
triggering a burst of innate immunity at the expense of host fitness. 
Our findings reveal a regulatory layer that links adaptive and innate 
immunity in bacteria, underscoring CRISPR–Cas as a coordinator of 
defence networks and highlighting the immunity guard theme for 
prokaryotes.
Defences embedded within I-C CRISPR–Cas
Anti-phage defence systems have been found encoded within or near 
others, a property that facilitated their systematic discovery5. We 
noticed that some known defence systems are occasionally embed-
ded within type I-C CRISPR–Cas loci. For example, Sir2–HerA, Gabija 
and type IV Thoeris modules were observed within 12, 4 and 5 I-C loci, 
respectively, that are from diverse species of the Neisseria genus, as 
well as examples in Aggregatibacter and Haemophilus (Supplementary 
Table 1). Figure 1a illustrates three example ‘embedded’ modules that 
we experimentally characterized in this study.
The Sir2–HerA system (also known as Nezha) comprises a sirtuin 
(Sir2) NADase and a HerA helicase, which together form a supramolecu-
lar octadecamer with multiple enzymatic activities that are involved 
in phage defence19–21. Codon-optimized Sir2–HerA from Neisseria sub-
flava provide Escherichia coli cells with robust immunity against T2, T4, 
T5, T6 and virulent λ (λ-vir) phages (Extended Data Fig. 1a,b). Similarly,

Fig. 1 | Three known phage defence systems embedded in and regulated  
by type I-C CRISPR–Cas. a, Sir2–HerA, Gabija and type IV Thoeris systems 
embedded within three type I-C CRISPR–Cas loci. b, The 5′ and 3′ termini of the 
SRU-encoded crlRNAs as determined by sRNA-seq (see Supplementary Fig. 1 
for actual DNA sequences). The sizes of ΨS and flanking repeat-derived 5′ and  
3′ handles on each mature crlRNA are indicated. Black arrowheads show Cas5 
cleavage sites; red arrowheads indicate non-canonical processing sites within 
the ΨR2 stem loop. c, Nucleotide identity between each crlRNA and its cognate 
target, with the nearby −10 and −35 promoter elements of the embedded 
immune system shown. 5′-TTC-3′ PAMs for I-C CRISPR–Cas are highlighted in 
red. Blue nucleotides are mutated in e–g. d, Experimental design to evaluate 
crlRNA-mediated regulation in Neisseria. The shuttle reporter vector carries 
the gonococcal plasmid replication origin (pFP10 ori), cas5–cas8–cas7 operon, 
SRU and a nano-luciferase driven by the defence system promoter, all on pACYC. 
Luminescence was measured to assess the strength of each crlRNA-targeted 
promoter. e–g, Regulatory effects of wild-type (WT) or mutated (Mut) crlRNAs 
on their corresponding Sir2–HerA (e), Gabija (f) and type IV Thoeris (g) target 
promoter in the presence or absence of I-C Cas proteins. Data are presented  
as mean ± s.d. from three biological replicates. P values were calculated using  
a two-sided Student’s t-test. Note that the Thoeris promoter from Neisseria 
elongata subsp. glycolytica strain 431 was tested in this assay (Methods).

228  |  Nature  |  Vol 657  |  3 September 2026
Article
a CRISPR–Cas-embedded type IV Thoeris system from Actinobacillus 
equuli, encoding a Toll/interleukin-1 receptor (TIR)-domain protein and 
a prokaryotic caspase activated by TIR-generated signalling molecules22, 
protected E. coli against the non-model phage EP02SG (NCBI accession 
PQ301181) (Extended Data Fig. 1a,c). Thus, these modules are bona 
fide defence systems, and when expressed without the surrounding 
CRISPR–Cas context, they confer anti-phage protection to a non-native 
bacterial host.
All of these embedded immune systems are inserted between cas3 
and the I-C Cascade operon (cas5c, cas8c and cas7c) (Fig. 1a), sug-
gesting that this genomic configuration is favoured. Inspection of 
the intergenic regions (IGRs) revealed a conserved SRU preceding 
cas5c, which contains a spacer-like (ΨS) sequence flanked by two 
CRISPR repeat-like sequences (ΨR1 and ΨR2) (Fig. 1a,b). Small RNA 
sequencing (sRNA-seq) identified crlRNAs produced from these SRUs 
upon heterologous expression in E. coli (Fig. 1b and Supplementary 
Fig. 1). A canonical type I-C crRNA consists of an 11-nucleotide (nt) 5′ 
handle, a spacer sequence typically of 35 nt and a 21-nt 3′ handle23. 
These crlRNAs also exhibit a 11-nt 5′ handle, but carry shorter spac-
ers (25–27 nt) and variable 3′ handles. Whereas the primary crlRNA 
products associated with Gabija carry the canonical 21-nt 3′ handle, 
those associated with Sir2–HerA and Thoeris exhibit atypical 3′ han-
dles of 11–12 nt (Fig. 1b and Supplementary Fig. 1). Nevertheless, our 
in vitro experiments revealed canonical Cas5c cleavage sites on these 
crlRNA precursors (Extended Data Fig. 2), indicating subsequent 3′ 
end processing in vivo.
CrlRNAs repress embedded systems
The ΨS sequence of each crlRNA exhibits partial complementarity to 
the promoter of its associated defence system, with targets flanked 
by a canonical 5′-TTC-3′ I-C PAM24 (Fig. 1c). Across 21 crlRNAs, 16–18 
discontinuous base pairs typically formed within the 26–28-nt spacer 
sequence, with conserved mismatches at positions 6 and 12 relative 
to the PAM (Fig. 1c and Supplementary Table 1). Primer extension 
mapped the transcription start sites (TSSs) and promoter elements 
of each system (Extended Data Fig. 3). The crlRNA target overlaps the 
−10 element and TSS of the Gabija promoter (PgajA), and lies immedi-
ately upstream of the −35 elements of the Sir2–HerA (Psir2) and Tho-
eris (Ptir) promoters, suggesting that Cascade binding interferes with 
transcription. In E. coli cells expressing codon-optimized Cascade 
proteins from N. subflava CCUG 29761, the cognate crlRNA reduced 
Psir2-driven GFP expression by 21.3-fold compared with a spacer-
mutated, non-targeting control (Extended Data Fig. 4a). When we 
introduced compensatory mutations into Psir2 to restore base pair-
ing, the mutated crlRNA, but not the wild-type counterpart, achieved 
transcriptional inhibition. Electrophoretic mobility shift assay (EMSA) 
further confirmed that Neisseria Cascade complex loaded with the 
Sir2–HerA-associated crlRNA specifically binds to Psir2 DNA (Extended 
Data Fig. 4b,c). Collectively, these results suggest that embedded 
defence systems are transcriptionally repressed by crlRNA-guided 
Cascade.
Next, we tested whether crlRNAs mediate similar inhibitory effects 
in a more native Neisseria host. We constructed a shuttle vector by 
inserting a gonococcal replication origin (pFP10 ori)25 into the E. coli 
pACYC plasmid and introduced it into Neisseria mucosa ATCC 19696 
by electroporation (Fig. 1d). For each of the three defence system pro-
moters, we assessed their crlRNA-mediated inhibition in this strain 
by measuring luminescence from the nano-luciferase transcriptional 
reporter (Fig. 1d). All 3 promoters were potently inhibited by their cor-
responding crlRNAs, with fold reductions of 48.5 (Psir2), 3.8 (PgajA) and 
8.7 (Ptir), respectively (Fig. 1e–g). This inhibition depended on sequence 
complementarity between the crlRNAs and promoters, and the pres-
ence of Cas proteins (Fig. 1e–g), confirming transcriptional regulation 
by crlRNA-guided Cascade.
Diverse CRISPR–Cas-supervised defences
We next examined the diversity of immune systems that are embedded 
and regulated by type I-C CRISPR–Cas. Analysis of cas3–cas5c IGRs from 
diverse bacteria identified six additional putative systems, tentatively 
designated CRISIS1–6 (Fig. 2a and Supplementary Table 1). Each CRISIS 
system is followed by an SRU that is predicted to generate a crlRNA 
partially complementary to a PAM-flanked sequence upstream of the 
CRISIS genes (Fig. 2a). As observed for validated systems, these crl-
RNAs typically form 16–18 discontinuous base pairs with conserved 
mismatches at positions 6 and 12, although additional mismatches 
were frequently observed for CRISIS2- and CRISIS5-associated crlRNAs 
(Supplementary Table 1). Our sRNA-seq analyses of CRISIS1–4 further 
revealed predominant 49–50-nt crlRNAs with conserved 11-nt 5′ handles 
and approximately 12-nt 3′ handles, resembling those that regulate 
Sir2–HerA and Thoeris (Extended Data Fig. 5).
Primer extension identified the TSS for each CRISIS system, which lies 
37–43 bp downstream of the target site of the cognate crlRNA (Fig. 2a). 
With this information, we conducted reporter assays using GFP in 
E. coli and nano-luciferase in N. mucosa. All CRISIS promoters tested 
were effectively inhibited by the corresponding crlRNA, with fold reduc-
tions ranging from 4.79 to 60.22 in E. coli (Extended Data Fig. 4d), and 
from 3.17 to 10.80 in N. mucosa (Fig. 2b). Overall, the CRISIS5 and CRI-
SIS2 promoters consistently showed the highest and lowest level of 
inhibition, respectively. Together, CRISIS1–6 systems are also subjected 
to crlRNA- and Cascade-mediated repression in both the native Neis-
seria context and in a heterologous E. coli host.
Beyond individual immune systems, we also identified compos-
ite defensive gene clusters embedded within type I-C CRISPR–Cas, 
designated SMRD for the four core genes encoding a SNF2-related 
protein, a DNA methyltransferase, a RhuM family virulence protein 
and a DEAD-box helicase. SMRD clusters usually contain additional 
defensive modules such as CRISIS2, Kiwa, or a combination of one 
nucleoid-associated protein (NAP) and two hypothetical proteins 
(Fig. 2c and Supplementary Table 1). Each SMRD gene cluster is fol-
lowed by an SRU targeting its putative promoter, and a GFP reporter 
assay in E. coli confirmed a 9.2-fold crlRNA-guided inhibition of the 
promoter of the NAP-encoding SMRD cluster (Extended Data Fig. 4e). 
These findings expand the scope of CRISPR–Cas-supervised immunity 
to include composite multiple-system defence clusters.
Finally, a comprehensive informatic search of all cas3–cas5c inter-
genic sequences from the NCBI database uncovered another 11 putative 
CRISIS systems (Supplementary Table 2), highlighting the diversity of 
CRISIS systems that are yet to be characterized. Phylogenetic analysis 
revealed that these CRISIS-encoding I-C loci, which represent around 
17.9% of total I-C loci, are distributed across multiple evolutionary 
branches (Fig. 2d). This non-clustered distribution implies that these 
innate immune systems have separately inserted in between cas3 and 
cas5c, a genetic hotspot for embedding, during the diversification of 
I-C CRISPR–Cas.
CRISIS utilizes diverse antiviral domains
To assess the function of CRISIS, we synthesized CRISIS1–6 genes from 
two bacterial species each (Fig. 3a) and expressed them in E. coli under 
inducible promoters. CRISIS1–5 conferred resistance to at least one of 
nine tested phages (Fig. 3a and Extended Data Fig. 6), although phage 
specificity varied between homologues from different organisms. We 
did not detect defence activity for the tested CRISIS6 systems, possi-
bly owing to expression limitations or incompatibility with the tested 
phage panel. However, a ‘standalone’ homologue of CRISIS6 that is not 
CRISPR–Cas-associated protected E. coli against T2 and T4 phages, 
supporting a role for this family in defence (Extended Data Fig. 7a).
We next investigated the functional components or domains of 
each system. CRISIS1, CRISIS5 and CRISIS6 are single-gene systems. 

Nature  |  Vol 657  |  3 September 2026  |  229
CRISIS6 encodes a protein containing only a detectable Sir2-like 
domain that is capable of degrading NAD+ (ref. 22) (Extended Data 
Fig. 7b), and was therefore named sole-Sir2. Indeed, biochemical 
reactions and liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry (LC–MS) 
confirmed that the ‘standalone’ homologue of sole-Sir2 actively con-
verts NAD+ to ADP-ribose (Extended Data Fig. 7c). CRISIS5 encodes 
a two-transmembrane (2TM) protein that DefenceFinder26 recog-
nizes as an AbiZ homologue; we therefore call it AbiZ-like. Despite 
negligible sequence or structural similarity to canonical AbiZ27,28, 
both proteins contain a long α-helix that separates the C-terminal 
2TM domain from unrelated N-terminal domains (Supplementary 
Fig. 2a). CRISIS1 (also known as Wukong), encodes a large protein 
with an N-terminal PHP exonuclease domain and a C-terminal ATPase 
domain. Notably, inactivating either domain abolishes anti-phage 
activity (Fig. 3b).
CRISIS2 and CRISIS4 are two-gene systems. CRISIS2 was previously 
named PD-T4-4 (ref. 29), and comprises an ATPase and a hypotheti-
cal protein, both of which are indispensable for its immune function 
(Fig. 3c). Mutating the predicted ATPase active site also abolishes 
defence, underscoring the requirement for ATP hydrolysis (Fig. 3c).

===== Holley2026_Nature_IcelandicPangenome =====

Nature  |  www.nature.com  |  1
Article
An Icelandic pangenome reference
Guillaume Holley1,6 ✉, Hannes P. Eggertsson1,6 ✉, Snaedis Kristmundsdottir1, Doruk Beyter1, 
Astros Th Skuladottir1,2, Kristjan H. S. Moore1, Pall I. Olason1, Arnaldur Gylfason1, 
Olafur T. Magnusson1, Asmundur Oddsson1, Hreinn Stefansson1, Agnar Helgason1,3, 
Gisli Masson1, Patrick Sulem1, Daniel F. Gudbjartsson1,4, Kari Stefansson1,2,7 & 
Bjarni V. Halldorsson1,5,7
Reference bias is an issue that affects most genomic studies analysing short reads 
mapped to a reference genome1,2. It can be mitigated by mapping to multiple 
haplotypes represented in a pangenome3–5. Here we introduce two new methods to 
address reference bias: Emblask for pangenome construction and Weaver for mapping 
to pangenomes at scale. Emblask is a hybrid long- and short-read haplotype-resolved 
dual assembly pipeline for parent–offspring trio data. Using Emblask, we assembled 
698 Icelandic haplotypes and added them to the Human Pangenome Reference 
Consortium (HPRC) pangenome4 to construct an Icelandic pangenome reference 
(HPRC-ICE) including 51.41 million small variants. We mapped the short reads of 57,630 
Icelanders to HPRC-ICE with Weaver and called 98.96 million variants, representing a 
6.17% increase over mapping to a linear reference. We uncovered new variants in low-
mappability regions, including a pathogenic single nucleotide polymorphism (SNP) 
in GBA1 that associates with early onset Parkinson’s disease and a missense SNP in CBS 
that is pathogenic for homocystinuria. We replicated the GBA1 association in the UK 
Biobank6 with a targeted remapping of 429,193 British and Irish participants.
Analyses of human genetic sequences7–9 are predominantly based on 
short reads mapped to a single reference genome10,11, resulting in an 
inaccurate or an incomplete mapping owing to gaps in the reference 
sequences and a strong bias towards the reference allele1,2. The current 
human reference genome11 (GRCh38) has been updated several times 
since its initial release with alternate contigs that represent chromo-
somal sequences diverging from its primary scaffolds. Although those 
additional sequences contribute to more diversity in GRCh38, they are 
often discarded from genomics analysis because of their similarity 
with primary scaffolds, which often leads to mapping ambiguity and 
in turn decreased variant calling sensitivity. Furthermore, GRCh38 
is still an incomplete representation of the human genome, as its 
primary scaffolds contain about 151 Mb of gaps and 9 Mb of errone-
ously duplicated or collapsed regions12. The recent advance of long 
read sequencing13 has enabled the first gapless assembly of a human 
genome14, T2T-CHM13, comprising near error-free assemblies of the 
22 autosomes and chromosome X, later complemented by a chromo-
some Y assembly15. Although T2T-CHM13 adds more than 182 Mb of 
sequence with respect to GRCh38, including 99 novel protein-coding 
genes, it does not capture any sequence differences between haplo-
types or individuals.
Computational pangenomics is a field of study dedicated to the 
joint analysis and usage of genomic sequence collections over tradi-
tional linear references3,16–22. To this end, the HPRC has sequenced the 
genomes of 47 individuals from various ethnic groups and assembled 
them into contiguous high-quality haplotype-resolved assemblies4. The 
genetic variations of these individuals have been extracted from their 
assemblies and combined into a pangenome represented as a graph 
(Methods). Overall, the HPRC pangenome contributes an additional 
90 Mb of non-reference sequences derived from structural variants 
and 119 Mb of euchromatic sequences with respect to GRCh38. Despite 
this, widespread adoption of pangenomes has been hindered due to 
their structural complexity, usage of new file formats and more com-
putational requirements than required for linear reference mapping.
Here we used the Emblask pipeline to assemble 698 high-quality 
Icelandic haplotypes and combined them with the HPRC assemblies 
to create the HPRC-ICE pangenome reference, composed of 788 hap-
lotypes (Fig. 1). Weaver maps to pangenome references composed of 
hundreds of haplotypes, such as HPRC-ICE, and outputs read map-
pings in a linear coordinate system such as GRCh38 or T2T-CHM13. 
As Weaver uses existing file formats, it can simply replace any linear 
reference mapper in existing analysis pipelines. The linear coordinate 
system built into the pangenome reference enables direct access to 
available genome annotations and enables comparisons with linear 
reference callsets. We showed that Weaver mapping to the HPRC-ICE 
on a cohort of 57,630 Icelanders resulted in a 6.17% increase of reliably 
called variants over linear mapping, including variants that associ-
ate with disease, demonstrating the recovery of variation previously 
camouflaged by reference bias.
Emblask: hybrid dual assembly pipeline
Emblask is a diploid genome assembly pipeline that produces two hap-
lotype assemblies, also called dual assembly, from parent–offspring 
trio data. Unlike most haplotype-resolved dual assembly tools, which 
rely primarily on accurate long reads23,24, Emblask is a hybrid approach

2  |  Nature  |  www.nature.com
Article
that uses both noisy long reads of the offspring and accurate short reads 
of all three trio members, specifically Oxford Nanopore Technologies 
(ONT) R9.4 and paired-end Illumina reads in this study.
Emblask first uses Ratatosk25 to perform genome-wide and tar-
geted corrections of long reads using graphs built from the short 
reads as reference (Supplementary Methods 3). This reduces the 
long read error rate to less than 1%, while preserving the phase of each 
read. Next, the corrected long reads are assembled with Flye26 into 
haplotype-resolved sequences of unknown parental origin. At this 
stage, the assembly is incomplete and fragmented. The long reads are 
then realigned to the assembly, phased variant calling is performed 
with PEPPER-Margin-DeepVariant27 (PMDV) for error detection and 
filtering. The haplotype-resolved sequences and corrected long reads 
mapping to them are assigned to the paternal or maternal haplotypes 
on the basis of their similarity to the parental short reads. Finally, each 
set of haplotype-assigned corrected long reads is assembled and pol-
ished independently.
We evaluated Emblask with HG002 data and compared it to leading 
ONT R9.4 assembly pipelines. Our results show superior assembly qual-
ity and completeness (Q49.35 and 99.01%, respectively). Compared with 
variants called directly from mapped ONT R9.4, our HG002 assemblies 
yielded 1.34–9.76% more variants, longer phased regions (6.157 Mb 
switch NGC50) and more than 30% higher insertion–deletion (indel) 
accuracy (Supplementary Methods 7). We also assembled offspring 
haplotypes of 329 Icelandic trios using long reads with a mean error 
rate of 7% and short reads. The resulting 658 haplotype assemblies had 
1 error per 100,000 bases (Q50) and 98.9% completeness on average 
(Fig. 2a–c). Additional analysis of contiguity, BUSCO (benchmark-
ing universal single-copy orthologues) completeness, false duplica-
tions and phasing (Supplementary Methods 6) demonstrates that 
Emblask hybrid assembly substantially improves upon ONT R9.4-only 
dual assemblies28.
An Icelandic pangenome
We constructed an Icelandic pangenome reference (HPRC-ICE) from 
high-quality haplotype-resolved assemblies of Icelanders (ICE), assem-
blies of individuals with heterogeneous ancestries (HPRC), T2T-CHM13 
and GRCh38. The ICE set consists of 698 haplotype assemblies from 
361 Icelanders whose genomes were sequenced with Illumina short 
reads as well as PacBio HiFi (n = 49) or ONT R9.4 long reads (n = 312). 
PacBio and ONT–Illumina samples were assembled with hifiasm or 
hifiasm-trio23 and Emblask, respectively. The HPRC set includes 94 
high-quality haplotype assemblies (mean Q55.36 and 99.57% complete-
ness) from diverse populations4.
The HPRC-ICE pangenome reference is composed of a sequence 
graph initially built using a single reference assembly, GRCh38 in this 
study, to which the structural variants from T2T-CHM13 and 94 HPRC 
haplotype assemblies were incrementally added (Supplementary Meth-
ods 8). Since adding structural variants increases graph complexity and 
the HPRC assemblies were of higher quality and contiguity than the 
ICE assemblies, we chose not to add structural variants from Icelandic 
individuals to the graph. In addition, HPRC-ICE contains 51,418,313 
phased small variants from the ICE, HPRC and T2T-CHM13 haplotype

Fig. 1 | Overview of the study. Long and short reads from sequenced Icelandic 
individuals were used to create the Icelandic pangenome reference. For this 
purpose, we developed Emblask to assemble haplotype-resolved dual 
assemblies with ONT R9.4 and Illumina reads from parent–offspring trios. After 
quality control, 698 Icelandic haplotype assemblies were combined with the 
HPRC, GRCh38 and T2T-CHM13 assemblies to form the HPRC-ICE pangenome 
reference. We developed Weaver to map short reads of 57,630 Icelanders to the 
HPRC-ICE and subsequently joint-called 98.96 million small variants. We 
imputed these variants using the long-range phasing of 182,338 chip-genotyped 
Icelanders, resulting in 65.42 million well-imputing variants. WGS, whole- 
genome sequencing. Maps downloaded from https://mapsvg.com/ (CC0 1.0).

assemblies (n = 788). HPRC-ICE has about twice the number of small 
variants compared to the HPRC pangenome (Fig. 2d), accounting for 
66.7 Mb of inserted or deleted base pairs relative to GRCh38 and 39 Mb 
of substituted bases. Specifically, 75.87% (39,011,184) of the small vari-
ants are SNPs, 22.82% (11,735,493) are indels and 1.30% (671,636) are 
other types such as multiple nucleotide polymorphisms. Singleton vari-
ants represent 36.88% (14,388,640) of the SNPs and 38.87% (4,562,237) 
of the indels.
Pangenome mapping with Weaver
Weaver maps short reads to a pangenome reference and outputs align-
ments within the coordinates of the pangenome reference assembly 
sequence. The pangenome includes rare small variants whereas pre-
vious methods such as Giraffe17 and vg-map29 recommend excluding 
variants with allele frequency below 10% and 1%, respectively. Rare vari-
ants that are absent in the mapped sample may introduce ambiguity, 
adversely affect the mapping accuracy and increase mapping time30. 
Yet these variants may strongly affect the regulation of gene expression 
with significant clinical consequences31. We demonstrate that including 
rare variants in our mapping model enabled the discovery of pathogenic 
variants in Iceland and in the UK. The negative impact of rare variants 
in Giraffe can be mitigated by using personalized pangenome refer-
ences5 (GiraffePPR). This approach constructs personalized subgraphs 
incorporating variants across the entire frequency spectrum based on 
per-sample k-mer counts, thereby improving rare variant detection at 
the expense of storage and preprocessing computation.
Whereas several previous studies only included either structural 
variants or small variants in their pangenomes17,29,32, the data model 
used in Weaver represents small variants in phased variant call format33 
(VCF) and structural variants as a sequence graph in reference Graphi-
cal Fragment Assembly (rGFA) format16. This dichotomy has several 
benefits. First, sequence graphs are adapted to represent large and 
complex variants while VCF is widely used for small variant analysis. 
This separation avoids the pitfalls of loading all types of variants into a 
single index, which can drastically increase the structural complexity 
of the graph and cause longer running time, memory usage and disk 
footprints30. Second, generating a pangenome reference that includes 
all types of variants is a non-trivial problem that requires extensive 
computational resources as variants can be nested into larger ones21,34. 
However, pangenome construction can scale to hundreds of human 
haplotypes if restricted to non-nested small variants.

4  |  Nature  |  www.nature.com
Article
Weaver extends the traditional seed-and-extend mapping paradigm 
to pangenome graphs using minimizers (Supplementary Methods 2). 
Minimizers of the read sequence are anchored onto the graph to form 
seeds (Fig. 3a,b). Vertices in the graph have a rank corresponding to the 
haplotype assembly that they are a sub-sequence of. Adjacent seeds 
of a read are chained together if they map along a path of the same 
rank (Fig. 3c). A chain is progressively extended by traversing paths 
of the same rank as the chain and aligning the read to the sequences 
spelled out by these paths. The alignment score of a chain considers 
all of the phased small variants that overlap the same path as the chain 
(Fig. 3d). The highest-scoring alignment is selected as the primary 
sequence.
Weaver improves variant calling
We benchmarked Weaver against BWA-MEM35 (v0.7.16a), DRAGEN20 
(v4.2.4) and two Giraffe17 (v.1.51.0) workflows (Giraffe and GiraffePPR), 
reporting only the better-performing Giraffe workflow for each 
experiment. We used the most recently published versions of each 
method. Mapping performance was evaluated on simulated short 
reads from both haplotypes of the T2T-Q100 HG002 dual assembly36 
(Supplementary Methods 9). Compared with Giraffe, DRAGEN and 
BWA-MEM, Weaver mapped more reads correctly (Fig. 4a), particularly 
in the Genome in a Bottle (GIAB)-stratified low-mappability regions37 
(Fig. 4b).

Fig. 3 | Weaver seed-and-extend mapping algorithm. a, A pangenome 
reference composed of a graph constructed from three haplotype sequences 
(HG38, CHM13 and HPRC#42) and small variants of three other haplotype 
sequences (ICE#1–3). Minimizers of length 3 are extracted from the sequences 
in the graph and the ICE#1–3 haplotype sequences. They are added to a seed 
index along with their graph location. SVs, structural variants. b, Minimizers m1 
to m7 are extracted from a read sequence and anchored onto the graph using 
the seed index. c, Chains c1, c2, c3 and c4 are formed by linking together adjacent 
seeds in the read that match a path of same rank in the graph. d, The longest 
chain or chains (c1 shown) are extended by walking paths in the graph with same 
rank as the chain and aligning the spelled-out sequences to the read sequence.
